Deriving a GENERIC system from a Hamiltonian system
Alexander Mielke, Mark A. Peletier, Johannes Zimmer

Abstract
We reconsider the fundamental problem of coarse-graining infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian dynamics to obtain a macroscopic system which includes dissipative mechanisms. In particular, we study the thermodynamical implications concerning Hamiltonians, energy, and entropy and the induced geometric structures such as Poisson and Onsager brackets (symplectic and dissipative brackets). We start from a general finite-dimensional Hamiltonian system that is coupled linearly to an infinite-dimensional heat bath with linear dynamics. The latter is assumed to admit a compression to a finite-dimensional dissipative semigroup (i.e., the heat bath is a dilation of the semigroup) describing the dissipative evolution of new macroscopic variables. Already in the finite-energy case (zero-temperature heat bath) we obtain the so-called GENERIC structure (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible Irreversible Coupling), with conserved energy, nondecreasing entropy, a new Poisson structure, and an Onsager operator describing the dissipation. However, their origin is not obvious at this stage. After extending the system in a natural way to the case of positive temperature, giving a heat bath with infinite energy, the compression property leads to an exact multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process that drives the rest of the system. Thus, we are able to identify a conserved energy, an entropy, and an Onsager operator (involving the Green-Kubo formalism) which indeed provide a GENERIC structure for the macroscopic system. Keywords and phrases: Hamiltonian systems, gradient systems, GENERIC systems, coarse-graining, heat bath, temperature, Gaussian measures, multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, dilations, compressions, energy, entropy, Poisson operator, Onsager operator, Caldeira-Leggett AMS Mathematics Subject Classification: 37K06, 37L05, 37D35, 80A05, 82C35



1
Introduction

1.1
Coarse-graining,
GENERIC
, and compressions

We revisit the classical topic of coarse-graining of Hamiltonian systems, and derive a thermodynamically consistent reduction for Hamiltonian systems which linearly couple a finite-dimensional Hamiltonian system to an infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian system acting as heat bath. A crucial element in the reduction procedure is the theory of compressions. We now describe the main concepts in more detail.

Coarse-graining
is the process by which less detailed (‘coarse-grained’) models of physical systems are
derived from more detailed ones (‘fine-grained’; see, e.g.,
[
E11
,
Kue15
]
). One methodology for
coarse-graining is the Mori-Zwanzig formalism, which allows to give macroscopic descriptions of microscopic
systems such as those arising in molecular dynamics. In particular, this allows one to study the emergence of
macroscopic dissipation from microscopically reversible Hamiltonian systems. In this paper we study a
classical example of this type; see
[
JaP98
,
Zwa73
]
and also the
monograph
[
Zwa01
]
for related treatments.

GENERIC
describes a class of thermodynamic evolution equations
that is formulated in terms on an energy and an entropy functional and two
geometric structures for the Hamiltonian and the dissipative dynamics. The
origins of such systems goes back to
[
Grm84
,
Grm85
]
and the
metriplectic theory developed in
[
Mor84
,
Mor86
,
Mor09
]
,
whereas the name
General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible Irreversible
Coupling
and the acronym
GENERIC
were introduced in
[
GrÖ97
,
ÖtG97
]
. The
GENERIC
framework provides a
general
way to write down thermodynamically
consistent
equations
for the coupling of two ideal systems, namely
a
reversible
(or Hamiltonian) system and an
irreversible
(or
dissipative) system.
GENERIC
systems form a subclass of all thermodynamically
consistent systems that allow for a better understanding of the interaction
between the two different phenomena of reversible and irreversible dynamics.
In particular, it will help us to see how dissipative systems can be obtained
via coarse graining of a purely Hamiltonian systems.

If an evolution equation can be put in
GENERIC
form, then it is automatically
thermodynamically consistent. In particular, it satisfies conservation of an
energy and monotonicity of an entropy. In
GENERIC
, this thermodynamic
consistency is implemented by combining a symplectic operator acting on the
energy and a semidefinite operator acting on the entropy. In addition, the
book
[
Ött05
]
describes a Mori-Zwanzig-type derivation of equations of

GENERIC
type from microscopic Hamiltonian systems with a clear separation of
time scales. In this paper we derive a
GENERIC
form for the coarse-grained
evolution, and this allows us to give an interpretation for the various
components of the
GENERIC
structure.

A key ingredient in this mathematically rigorous derivation is the third component,
compressions
, which can be thought of the reverse of dilations. A
dilation
of a semigroup
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}
on a space
{\mathbf{Y}}

is a unitary group
{\mathbb{U}}_{t}
on a larger Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}
whose projection onto
{\mathbf{Y}}
coincides with

{\mathbb{S}}_{t}

[
NF∗10
]
.
Conversely, compressions yield a projection of a unitary group on a large space
onto a dissipative semigroup on a subspace. It is the existence of a suitable compression subspace that allows us to write the Mori-Zwanzig reduction in Markovian form and relate the dissipative part to the fluctuations.

1.2
Sketch of the microscopic Hamiltonian system

We first describe the starting point of this paper, which we call the ‘microscopic’ system. Later, from Section
1.4
onwards, we will describe how we apply a coarse-graining map to the state space of this system, which will lead to a system that we call ‘macroscopic’. The terms ‘microscopic’ and ‘macroscopic’ do not refer to actual space or time scales, but are relative to the operation of coarse-graining. The coarse-graining map is highly non-injective, and therefore the macroscopic system has fewer degrees of freedom than the microscopic one.

The starting point of this paper, the ‘microscopic system’, resembles well-known models in the
literature (see,
e.g.,
[
JaP98
,
KS∗02
,
Rey06
]
). It couples
two separate Hamiltonian systems: System A is finite-dimensional, and System B
is infinite-dimensional and is interpreted as a heat bath. System A may be
nonlinear, while System B and the coupling are linear. This is illustrated in
Figure
1.1
. The whole system is Hamiltonian, and in the interesting cases, the system also is reversible in time. The evolution is
deterministic, while the initial data for the heat bath may be random, as we discuss below.

System A
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
coupling
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}=\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
System B
=
heat bath
({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})

Figure 1.1:
The microscopic system is a coupling of a fixed Hamiltonian system
(‘System A’) and a heat bath (‘System B’). See
Section
1.2
for a discussion.

System A is described by a triple
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
{\mathbf{Z}}
is a state space,

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a Hamiltonian, and
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\colon{\mathbf{Z}}\to{\mathbf{Z}}
denotes the Poisson (also called
co-symplectic) operators. We make the assumption that
\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits_{z}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
, corresponding to the Lebesgue measure being invariant (see Remark
4.7
). The evolution of System A alone is given by the Hamiltonian evolution equation

\dot{z}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
.

System B, the heat bath, is described by a similar triple,

({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
, where
{\mathbf{H}}
is a Hilbert space,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}
the Hamiltonian of the heat
bath and
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
a symplectic evolution operator. The evolution of the heat bath is linear. Specifically,
we take

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\quad\text{for all }\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}.

The coupling between system A and system B is described by a Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, which
couples the two systems linearly by

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(z,\eta)=\left({\mathbb{A}}z\middle|{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}},

(1.1)

where
\left(\cdot\middle|\cdot\right)_{{\mathbf{H}}}
is the inner product in
{\mathbf{H}}
. Here

{\mathbb{A}}\colon{\mathbf{Z}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
is a (linear) embedding operator;
{\mathbb{P}}
is an
orthogonal projection operator that is specified later. Since
{\mathbb{P}}
is
self-adjoint, we can alternatively define
{\mathsf{C}}:={\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{A}}\colon{\mathbf{Z}}\to{\mathbf{H}}

and write (
1.1
) as

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(z,\eta)=\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}.

(1.2)

The operator
{\mathsf{C}}
satisfies
{\mathsf{C}}={\mathbb{P}}{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}={\mathsf{C}}^{*}{\mathbb{P}}
.

The bilinearity of (
1.2
) is assumed for simplicity; we expect that similar results can be
proved also for functionals that are nonlinear in
z
but linear in
\eta
, e.g., the model studied
in
[
Zwa73
]
.

The
full (coupled) microscopic evolution for
(z,\eta)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
is governed
by the Hamiltonian

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z,\eta)={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(z,\eta),

(1.3)

together with the joint symplectic operator
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\oplus{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
The corresponding evolution equations are

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\big({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z_{t})+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta_{t}\big),

(1.4a)

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\,\big(\eta_{t}+{\mathsf{C}}z_{t}\big),

(1.4b)

and the full Hamiltonian system is denoted by the triple

({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
. The
aim of this paper is to coarse-grain the coupled system
(
1.4
) to a system where the macroscopic observable

z\in{\mathbf{Z}}
is maintained, but the infinite-dimensional degree of freedom

\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}
is reduced to a finite-dimensional coupling variable
w
. The
so-called compression property (see Assumption
1.2
below) will
imply that despite the loss of information the resulting system in

(z,w)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}
still is Markovian.

Example 1.1

(Running example, part 1)
.

To facilitate the understanding we provide a more explicit example for the
whole theory that explains all the constructions in a concrete case.

For System A we consider a canonical Hamiltonian system with

z=(q,p)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}={\mathbf{Z}},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(q,p)=\frac{1}{2}|p|^{2}+V(q),\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&{\mathbb{I}}_{n{\times}n}\\
{-}{\mathbb{I}}_{n{\times}n}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

For System B we choose

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{3}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|^{2}_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-\partial_{x}&0&0\\
0&-\partial_{x}&0\\
0&0&-\partial_{x}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

In particular, the unitary group
(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is the group of
shifts
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}f:=f(\,\cdot\,{-}t)
. For simplicity we assume in this
example that coupling to the heat bath occurs only via the
state variable
q
(but not the momentum
p
), i.e.
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q,p,\eta)=\left({\mathsf{K}}q\middle|\eta\right)
for a linear bounded operator

{\mathsf{K}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbf{H}}
.

In summary, the example system has state
(q,p,\eta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}^{n}{\times}{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
and is a coupled system of a nonlinear ODE
and a linear transport equation for the three components of
\eta
:

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}q\\
p\\
\eta\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&{\mathbb{I}}_{n\times n}&0\\
\!\!{-}{\mathbb{I}}_{n\times n}&0&0\\
0&0&\!\!{-}\partial_{x}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{D}}V(q)+{\mathrm{D}}_{q}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q,\eta)\\
p\\
\eta+{\mathrm{D}}_{\eta}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q,\eta)\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
\!\!-{\mathrm{D}}V(q)-{\mathsf{K}}^{*}\eta\\[3.00003pt]
-\partial_{x}(\eta+{\mathsf{K}}q)\end{array}\right).

The first two equations are ODEs in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, whereas the third equation is a
transport equation in
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
that has to be interpreted in a
suitable weak or mild form,
if we want to treat general initial conditions (see the abstract construction
in Theorem
2.5
).

∎

1.3
The compression property

We next choose the projection operator
{\mathbb{P}}
and its range
{\mathbf{W}}
. The space
{\mathbf{W}}
needs to be large enough to contain the range of
{\mathsf{C}}
, but may also be larger. The central requirement that determines the choice of
{\mathbf{W}}
and the projection
{\mathbb{P}}
onto
{\mathbf{W}}
is the following ‘compression’ property.

Assumption 1.2

(Compression property of the heat bath)
.

There exists a linear operator
{\mathbb{D}}
on
{\mathbf{W}}
such that

{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\big|_{\mathbf{W}}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}&t\geq 0\\
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}&t\leq 0\end{cases}\qquad\text{on }{\mathbf{W}}.

(1.5)

The eigenvalues of
{\mathbb{D}}+{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
are strictly positive.

The left-hand side in the identity (
1.5
) should be interpreted
as follows. If we evolve the heat bath starting from
w\in{\mathbf{W}}
, the result

\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}w
typically is not an element of
{\mathbf{W}}
. Then
{\mathbb{P}}
on the
left-hand side projects
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}w
back onto
{\mathbf{W}}
, as one does in
the Mori-Zwanzig framework. In particular, with the compression property the
flow map
w\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}w
is equivalent to the memory-less
evolution
\dot{w}=-{\mathbb{D}}w
for
t\geq 0
.

Assumption
1.2
in fact implies that
{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
is a
strictly dissipative semigroup, since

\big\lVert\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}w\big\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha t}\|w\|_{\mathbf{H}}\qquad\text{for all $t\geq 0$ and $w\in{\mathbf{W}}$},

where
\alpha>0
is the smallest eigenvalue of
({\mathbb{D}}+{\mathbb{D}}^{*})/2
.

Remark 1.3
.

In Assumption
1.2
, it is equivalent to assume only the
behavior for
t\geq 0
: the unitary nature of
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
then implies that

{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\right)^{*}{\mathbb{P}}=\left({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}}\right)^{*}=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}{\mathbb{P}}
for
t\leq 0
.

∎

The context of the compression property is the theory of (dilations and)
compressions. The relevant background is summarised in
Appendix
C
. In essence, the theory of dilations
states that for any contraction semigroup
(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}
one can find a
unitary group
(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on a sufficiently larger Hilbert space such
that
C_{t}
is a ‘coarse-grained version’ of
S_{t}
. Conversely, one can in this
setting regard the dissipative semigroup as a compression of a given unitary
group. In our context, the unitary group is
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
and represents the
linear Hamiltonian system and
\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
is the contraction semigroup. We
interpret a compression as a coarse-graining of a unitary group.

The crucial implication of the Compression Property
1.2
is that
the evolution
\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
on
{\mathbf{W}}
is a semigroup. It is thus in particular
Markovian in the sense that the evolution equation does not involve the
past. In (
2.5b
), we give a representation of the heat bath
variable
\eta
which involves a memory term. The compression property allows
us to rewrite that memory term as a local term.

For the coupled system, the projection onto
{\mathbf{W}}
, i.e., the part of the heat
bath which interacts with System A, yields a compression semigroup. The
existence of a compression, and with it the associated compression subspace

{\mathbf{W}}
is related to the theory of Markovian subspaces in linear systems
theory, see
[
LiP15
, Ch. 10]
. Necessary and sufficient conditions
for the existence of compression subspaces are given
in
[
Pic86
, Th. 5.4]
. We discuss the Compression
Property
1.2
in more detail in Section
3
.

Remark 1.4

(The compression property replaces ergodicity)
.

In finite-dimensional Hamiltonian systems, dissipative behavior is often associated with ergodic
properties. In the context of the infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian heat bath of this paper, the Compression
Property
1.2
mimics the property of ergodic systems. In the rest of this paper, we indeed
show that the resulting system has thermodynamically consistent dissipative behavior.

Example 1.5

(Running example, part 2)
.

Example
1.1
was chosen such that the unitary shift group

{\mathbb{S}}_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
admits an explicit and simple compression. We
choose a linear operator
{\mathbb{D}}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with eigenvalues
\vartheta_{1}>0
and

\vartheta_{2}\pm{\mathrm{i}}\varsigma
with
\vartheta_{2},\varsigma>0
, and define the
functions
f_{j}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
by

\displaystyle f_{1}(y)=\frac{\chi_{{}_{\leq}}\!(y)\mathrm{e}^{\vartheta_{1}y}}{\textstyle\sqrt{2\vartheta_{1}}}\!\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}1\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right)\!,\ f_{2}(y)=\frac{\chi_{{}_{\leq}}\!(y)\mathrm{e}^{\vartheta_{2}y}}{\sqrt{2\vartheta_{2}}}\!\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}0\\
\cos(\varsigma y)\\
\sin(\varsigma y)\end{array}\right)\!,\ f_{3}(y)=\frac{\chi_{{}_{\leq}}\!(y)\mathrm{e}^{\vartheta_{2}y}}{\sqrt{2\vartheta_{2}}}\!\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}0\\
\!{-}\sin(\varsigma y)\\
\cos(\varsigma y)\end{array}\right)\!,

where
\chi_{{}_{\leq}}=\bm{1}_{{]-\infty,0]}}
is the indicator function
for the negative part of the real line. We then define
{\mathbf{W}}
to be the
three-dimensional subspace
{\mathbf{W}}=\mathrm{span}\big\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\big\}
, and
the operator
{\mathbb{P}}
to be the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathbf{W}}
. Note that

\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
.

The validity of the compression property can roughly be seen as follows:
For
t>0
the functions
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}f_{j}=f_{j}(\,\cdot\,{-}t)
immediately leave

{\mathbf{W}}
, but they return to
{\mathbf{W}}
, if we cut them off at
y=0
by multiplying
with
\chi_{{}_{\leq}}
, i.e.
\chi_{{}_{\leq}}{\mathbb{S}}_{t}f_{j}\in{\mathbf{W}}
. Moreover, we can express
this action by the contraction semigroup
\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
, see the calculations in
the proof of Proposition
C.4
.

Using the orthonormal basis
\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
we can identify
{\mathbf{W}}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}

and using the orthogonal projections
{\mathbb{P}}
from
{\mathbf{H}}
to
{\mathbf{W}}
we find

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}=\begin{pmatrix}\vartheta_{1}&0&0\\
0&\vartheta_{2}&-\varsigma\\
0&\varsigma&\vartheta_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ \text{ and }

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\big|_{\mathbf{W}}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta_{1}t}&0&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta_{2}t}\cos(\varsigma t)&\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta_{2}t}\sin(\varsigma t)\\
0&-\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta_{2}t}\sin(\varsigma t)&\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta_{2}t}\cos(\varsigma t)\end{pmatrix}\text{ for }t\geq 0.

Having the explicit representation of
{\mathbf{W}}
, the coupling

{\mathsf{K}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
can be specified as

{\mathsf{K}}q=(\mathsf{c}^{1}{\cdot}q)f_{1}+(\mathsf{c}^{2}{\cdot}q)f_{2}+(\mathsf{c}^{3}{\cdot}q)f_{3}
,
where
\mathsf{c}^{1},\mathsf{c}^{2},\mathsf{c}^{3}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
are given coupling vectors. Hence,
the coupling energy reads

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(q,p,\eta)=\left({\mathsf{K}}q\middle|{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mathsf{c}^{j}{\cdot}q)\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!f_{j}(x){\cdot}\eta(x)\;\!\mathrm{d}x,

and the coupled ODE-PDE system takes the explicit form

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}q\\
p\\[3.00003pt]
\eta\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}p\\
\!\!-{\mathrm{D}}V(q)-\sum_{1}^{3}\int_{-\infty}^{0}f_{j}(y){\cdot}\eta(y)\;\!\mathrm{d}y\;\mathsf{c}^{j}\\[3.00003pt]
-\partial_{x}\big(\eta+\sum_{1}^{3}(\mathsf{c}^{j}{\cdot}q)f_{j}(\cdot)\big)\end{array}\right).

We see that the coupling between the ODEs (first two equations) and the linear
transport equation (third equation) occurs through the observable
functions
f_{j}\in{\mathbf{W}}
, which are exponentially decaying.

As mentioned above, the transport equation has to be interpreted in a suitable
weak or mild form if we want to treat general initial conditions. Note also
that the functions
f_{j}
do not lie in the domain of the generator
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
of
the unitary group
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
, because
\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})={\mathrm{H}}^{1}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})

and hence
{\mathbf{W}}\cap\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})=\{0\}
.

∎

1.4
Coarse-graining, part 1: Deterministic initial data

In Theorem
4.10
, we rigorously derive an equation for
a coarse-grained version of the microscopic system, and show how it can be
recognized as a
GENERIC
equation.
GENERIC
equations are evolution equations
for an unknown
y(t)\in{\mathbf{Y}}
that can be written in terms of two functionals

{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
and two operators
{\mathbb{J}}
and
{\mathbb{K}}
as

\dot{y}={\mathbb{J}}(y){\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{E}}(y)+{\mathbb{K}}(y){\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{S}}(y).

(1.6)

The quintuple
({\mathbf{Y}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},{\mathbb{J}},{\mathbb{K}})
is used to denote the
GENERIC
system. Here
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
are referred to as the energy and entropy
functionals, and
{\mathrm{D}}
is an appropriate concept of derivative (see
Remark
2.1
).
The operators
{\mathbb{J}}
and
{\mathbb{K}}
are called the
Poisson
and
Onsager
operators, and they define a Poisson bracket and a dissipative bracket.
In the
GENERIC
framework, the
components
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}
,
{\mathbb{J}}
, and
{\mathbb{K}}
are required to satisfy a
number of additional conditions, which in particular imply that along any
solution of (
1.6
) the functional
{\mathcal{E}}
is constant and the
functional
{\mathcal{S}}
is non-decreasing. We describe the
GENERIC
framework in
more detail in Section
4
.

To describe this result precisely we first define the coarse-graining map
\pi_{z,w,e}
,

{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}\ni(z,\eta)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{z,w,e}}}{{\longmapsto}}\ \left(\,z,\,w:={\mathbb{P}}\eta,\,e:=\tfrac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\|w\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}.

(1.7)

Thus, we apply the coarse-graining map to the coupled system

\big({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\big)
, which is an
infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian system, and obtain a coarse-grained system on
the reduced state space
{\mathbf{Y}}:={\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}
. This is done by
projecting out infinitely many degrees of freedom in
{\mathbf{H}}
and keeping only
the new observables
w={\mathbb{P}}\eta
in the finite-dimensional subspace

{\mathbf{W}}
of
{\mathbf{H}}
. Note
that System A is considered already as a ‘coarse-grained’ system, which is
preserved by
\pi_{z,w,e}
, and coupled to the new observable
w\in{\mathbf{W}}
. The third variable
e
allows us to recover
an important piece of information from the missing part

\eta-w=\eta-{\mathbb{P}}\eta
, namely its total energy

\tfrac{1}{2}\|\eta-w\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\|w\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
.
The variable
e
is only used to keep track of the energy exchanged with the
heat bath. Already for
(z,w)=(z,{\mathbb{P}}\eta)
we obtain the closed system of ODEs

\dot{z}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\big({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}w),\qquad\dot{w}=-{\mathbb{D}}w+{\mathsf{C}}z,

(1.8)

see Figure
1.2
. However, this reduced model does not have a
thermodynamical structure.

macro
micro
System of ODEs for
(z,w)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
coupling

w_{t}={\mathbb{P}}\eta_{t}\in{\mathbf{W}}
\dot{w}_{t}=-{\mathbb{D}}w_{t}{+}{\mathsf{C}}z_{t}
Hamiltonian system for
(z,\eta)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
coupling
\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
\bm{{\mathbb{P}}}

Figure 1.2:
The special coupling and the compression property lead to the closed
deterministic systems of ODEs (
1.8
) for
(z,w)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}
, where
w
is the observable part
w={\mathbb{P}}\eta
.

Using the additional scalar energy variable
e
, Part
1
of
Theorem
4.10
states the following. If
(z,\eta)
evolves as described in
Section
1.2
, starting from a given point
z(t=0)=z_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
and

\eta(t=0)=w_{0}\in{\mathbf{W}}
, then
y(t):=\big(z(t),w(t),e(t)\big):=\pi_{z,w,e}\big(z(t),\eta(t)\big)
is
an exact solution of the ordinary differential equation

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\begin{pmatrix}z\\
w\\
e\end{pmatrix}=\underbrace{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)&0&0\\
0&-{{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{skw}}}&0\\
0&0&0\end{array}\right)}_{{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)}\underbrace{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}w\\
w+{\mathsf{C}}z\\
1\end{array}\right)}_{{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)}\\
{}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\beta}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
0&{{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}&-{{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}(w{+}{\mathsf{C}}z)\\
0&-{\left({{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}(w{+}{\mathsf{C}}z)\middle|\Box\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}&\left({{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}(w{+}{\mathsf{C}}z)\middle|w{+}{\mathsf{C}}z\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\end{array}\right)}_{{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)}\underbrace{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
\beta\end{array}\right)}_{{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)}.

(1.9)

Here the operator
{\mathbb{D}}
is decomposed into symmetric and skew-symmetric parts,

{\mathbb{D}}={{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}+{{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{skw}}}\quad\text{with }{{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{sym}}}=\frac{1}{2}({\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*})\ \text{
and }\ {{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathrm{skw}}}:=\frac{1}{2}({\mathbb{D}}{-}{\mathbb{D}}^{*}),

(1.10)

and we have defined

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)

\displaystyle:={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|w\right)_{\mathbf{H}}+\frac{1}{2}\|w\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+e,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)

\displaystyle:=\beta e.

Here
\beta>0
is a parameter that will be discussed in Section
1.6
.

The result (
1.9
) of Theorem
4.10
is interesting
for a number of reasons. As we already noted in Section
1.3
, it is remarkable
that coarse-graining of this type leads to a closed system: typically, if in a dynamical system one disregards
a part of the state space, then unique solvability tends to break down. In this case the Compression
Property
1.2
guarantees that although knowledge of the initial values
z(0)
and
w(0)={\mathbb{P}}\eta(0)
is not enough to uniquely solve for the microscopic system

(z(t),\eta(t))
, it
does
suffice for the prediction of the
projected
version of

(z(t),\eta(t))
. This points to a special role of the compression property in the study of this type of
systems. The state space of the infinite-dimensional Hamiltonian system contains a finite-dimensional
manifold that is exactly invariant under the full flow, and reduction of the flow to the manifold is described
by a
GENERIC
equation.

Secondly, by following how the microscopic Hamiltonian-system components morph
through coarse-graining into the
GENERIC
equation (
1.9
) we can gain some understanding
about the
GENERIC
framework itself. In
Sections
4.4
,
4.5
,
and
4.6
, we use the results of this paper to shed
light on the modeling origin of the four components
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}
,
{\mathbb{J}}
,
and
{\mathbb{K}}
and the relations between them.

1.5
Coarse-graining, part 2: Heat bath with positive temperature

In Section
1.4
the elements
\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}
still have finite
energy
\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|^{2}
, and now we want to extend the theory to a heat bath
with positive temperature
1/\beta
, which also implies infinite energy. We first only look to
the interplay of the positive-temperature heat bath and the compression
property. This will explain basic features of our theory without too much
technicalities, because everything can be explained by Gaussian measures and
processes. In Section
1.6
we will then do the same
extension on the full Hamiltonian system
({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\otimes}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
.

For creating a Gaussian equilibrium measure corresponding to an energy
distribution “
\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}/2}\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta
” one embeds
{\mathbf{H}}
into
a bigger Hilbert space
{\mathbf{X}}
by a symmetric and positive definite trace-class
operator
{\mathbb{C}}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
such that
{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}{\mathbf{X}}
and

\|{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}=\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{X}}
. With this a Gaussian measure

\gamma_{\beta}
is defined such that the characteristic function

\chi={\mathcal{F}}\gamma_{\beta}
takes the form

\chi(\xi)=\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2\beta}\|{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}\xi\|_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\big)
. By the
general theory of Gaussian measures, it follows that the orthogonal projector

{\mathbb{P}}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
extends to a random variable
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
on the
space
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbf{X}},\gamma_{\beta})
, see
Section
2.2
. We show that
it is also possible to choose
{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
in such a way that the unitary
group
(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
extends by density to a strongly
continuous group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
{\mathbf{X}}
(see Section

C.2
). Thus, for each (infinite-energy) state

\eta\in{\mathbf{X}}
of the heat bath, we can define the process

t\mapsto Y_{t}(\eta):=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta\ \in{\mathbf{W}}.

It turns out that the process
Y
is completely independent of the choice of

{\mathbf{X}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
and is uniquely determined by the compression property
alone. Indeed, it is a stationary multivariate Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process with
mean
0\in{\mathbf{W}}
and covariance matrix

{\mathbb{E}}\big(Y_{t}{\otimes}Y_{s}\big)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s){\mathbb{D}}}&\text{for }t\geq s,\\[1.99997pt]
\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-(s-t){\mathbb{D}}^{*}}&\text{for }t\leq s.\end{cases}

see Lemma
3.2
. Hence, the paths
Y_{t}(\eta)
are the solutions of
the linear SDE

{\mathrm{d}}Y_{t}=-{\mathbb{D}}Y_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\Sigma\,{\mathrm{d}}B_{t}\quad\text{with }\Sigma=\big(\tfrac{\displaystyle 1}{\displaystyle\beta}\,({\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*})\big)^{1/2}.

See Figure
1.3
for an explanation how the drift term
and the noise term arise via the projection
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
and the
extended semigroup
\widehat{S}_{t}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
.

macro
micro
Stochastic process for
Y_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{W}}
{\mathbf{W}}
-valued
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
process
{\mathrm{d}}Y_{t}=-{\mathbb{D}}Y_{t}\;\!\mathrm{d}t+\Sigma{\mathrm{d}}B_{t}
Y_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s){\mathbb{D}}}\Sigma\;\!\mathrm{d}B_{s}
Linear Hamiltonian systems
positive temperature
1/\beta
zero temperature
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\infty},\gamma_{\beta})
\eta_{t}=\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}
extension
({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
\eta_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\eta_{0}
\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}|_{\mathbf{W}}
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}(I{-}\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\eta_{0}
​​

Figure 1.3:
The Gaussian measure
\gamma_{\beta}
encodes the temperature

1/\beta>0
. After extending
{\mathbb{P}}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
to

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
and
\widehat{S}_{t}
, respectively, the compression property implies that

t\mapsto Y_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}
defines a Gaussian process, namely the

{\mathbf{W}}
-valued Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process with drift
{\mathbb{D}}Y_{t}
and noise
intensity
\Sigma
with
\Sigma\Sigma^{*}=\frac{1}{\beta}({\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*})
.

In the following we discuss how this construction can be coupled to the
Hamiltonian System A.

1.6
Coarse-graining, part 3: Random initial data

In Section
1.4
we already derived a
GENERIC
structure for the case of a heat bath with zero temperature. But the result was ad
hoc and didn’t explain why this is the “correct form” of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
,

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
,
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
, and
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
. This question can only be answered
by looking at the coupled system at positive temperature
1/\beta>0
. This
means that we consider now initial conditions
(z_{0},\eta_{0})\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}

that are random according to a suitable equilibrium measure.

This approach will reveal the deeper connections to the coarse-graining of
systems with thermal fluctuations. For instance, it allows us to give a meaning
to the parameter
\beta>0
in (
1.9
) as an
inverse temperature and to ‘entropy’
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)=\beta e
as a
micro-canonical surface area. In addition, it addresses the slightly unnatural
initial datum of the previous section. There we started the heat bath at

\eta(t=0)=w_{0}\in{\mathbf{W}}
, which fixes the visible part of the bath to some
non-zero
w_{0}
, while requiring the (infinite-dimensional) ‘invisible’
component to be zero. This is an unnatural choice in a Hamiltonian system with a
Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
that gives no special
status to
{\mathbf{W}}
, and our choice of random initial data will also improve this
aspect.

We choose a distribution for the random initial data that builds on the
‘canonical’ invariant measure

\mu_{\beta}:=``{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z,\eta)}}\text{''}
of the
microscopic Hamiltonian system, where
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}
is the total
Hamiltonian given in (
1.3
). Like the Gaussian measure

\gamma_{\beta}
is defined on
{\mathbf{X}}\supset{\mathbf{H}}
, also
\mu_{\beta}
can be
rigorously defined on
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
but is no longer Gaussian unless

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is also quadratic. For any
(z_{0},w_{0})\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}

we then choose a random initial datum
(z_{0},\eta_{0})
from the conditional
measure
\mu_{\beta}(\,\cdot\,|z_{0},w_{0})
obtained by conditioning
\mu_{\beta}
to

z=z_{0}
and
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta=w_{0}
. This leads to an initial datum with
deterministic ‘visible’ part and random ‘invisible’ part in the heat bath. Once
the initial datum is selected, the microscopic Hamiltonian system generates a
deterministic evolution for
(z(t),\eta(t))
. This leads to a
SDE
for the
variables
(z,w)
on the space
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}
, see Figure
1.4
. As in
Section
1.5
it is the Gaussianity of
\gamma_{\beta}
and
the Compression Property
1.2
that guarantee
that the (Mori-Zwanzig type) coarse graining map

{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}\ni(z,\eta)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{z,w}}}{{\longmapsto}}\ (z,w:=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}\qquad\qquad\text{(see Section~\ref{ss:Microscopic-positive-temperature} for
$\widehat{\mathbb{P}}$)},

(1.11)

leads to a memory-less Markov process that can be described by a
SDE
.

macro
micro
Stochastic process for
(z,w)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
coupling
\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|w\right)_{\mathbf{W}}
w_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{t}\in{\mathbf{W}}
noise generated by

\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{t}
Hamilton. sys. for
(z,\eta)\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
or
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
coupling
\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\infty},\widehat{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
extension
({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
​
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}=I{-}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}

Figure 1.4:
The coarse-graining step, illustrated as the transition from the
bottom row to the top row. We separate the heat-bath variable
\eta
into the
observable part
w=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta
and the remainder
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta=\eta-\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta
. The pair

(z,w)
can be considered a stochastic process driven by
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta
.

Similarly to the deterministic case in Section
1.4
we also
need the ‘internal-energy’ variable
e
. For random initial data the formula

e=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|w\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
is not available, however,
since the Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
is almost
surely infinite (see Remark
2.2
). We circumvent
this problem by following an alternative but equivalent route: we first coarse-grain

(z,w)=\pi_{z,w}(z,\eta)=(z,\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta)
and then reconstruct
e

a posteriori
from
(z(t),w(t))
as the energy
exchanged with the heat bath.

The main result for this setup is the second part of Theorem
4.10
, which shows that the curve
(z(t),w(t),e(t))
constructed this way satisfies the following “
GENERIC

SDE
”:

{\mathrm{d}}\!\begin{pmatrix}z\\[-3.00003pt]
w\\[-3.00003pt]
e\end{pmatrix}=\Big[{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)+{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w){\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w,e)+\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w)\Big]\;\!\mathrm{d}t\\
+\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w)\;\!\mathrm{d}B(t),

(1.12)

where
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z)
,
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
are as in Section
1.4
, and the mobility
\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}
is given by

\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}(z,w):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\Sigma\\
-{\left(w{+}{\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\Sigma\,\square\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ with }\ \Sigma\Sigma^{*}=\frac{1}{\beta}({\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*}).

This equation is a natural stochastic counterpart of the deterministic GENERIC equation (
1.6
).
We discuss equations of this form in more detail in Section
4.2
.

Again, the derivation of (
1.12
) is interesting for a
number of reasons. As in the deterministic case, the fact that this is an

exact
reduction is remarkable: not only is the drift term
in (
1.12
) Markovian, as we already remarked in
Section
1.3
, but in addition the noise term is
exact Brownian noise, and therefore memoryless. Considering that the evolution
of the microscopic variable
(z,\eta)
is deterministic, and therefore
‘maximally memory-full’, this indeed is remarkable.

In addition, the mobility
\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}
that colors the noise is coupled to the Onsager operator
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
by the fluctuation-dissipation relation

\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}^{*}=\frac{2}{\beta}{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}.

This underscores the shared origin of
\bbSigma_{\mathrm{GEN}}
,
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
in the randomness of the initial data. We indeed show in Section
4.6
how
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
can be interpreted as the mass of a microcanonical measure, and how this leads to the corresponding drift
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
in (
1.6
) and (
1.12
).

1.7
Outline of this paper

The layout of this paper is as follows. The microscopic system is described in more detail in
Section
2
.

In Section
3
we employ a compression to derive the coarse-grained system. Specifically, in
Section
3.1
we introduce compressions and derive an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process on the projected space.
In Section
3.2

we perform the coarse-graining described in Sections
1.4
and
1.6

above, which leads to deterministic and stochastic evolutions. At this stage they still are without
GENERIC
structure.

In Section
4
we describe the
GENERIC
structure in detail and establish some properties of so-called
GENERIC
SDEs. By taking into account
the energy flowing in and out of the heat bath, as described in Sections
1.4
and
1.6
, we can formulate the equations obtained in Section
3.2
as a
GENERIC
system, either deterministic or stochastic, and identify the
components. This also gives insight in how the
GENERIC
restrictions arise in the coarse-graining
process.

In Section
5
we summarize the results of the paper, and discuss connections with various
other developments. In Appendix
A
we collect a number of properties of Gaussian
measures that we use, and in Appendix
B
we give the proofs of a number of lemmas.

List of notation, setting, and assumptions

We denote Hilbert spaces with boldface symbols and their elements with lower case letters. Below we will use Hilbert spaces
{\mathbf{H}}
,
{\mathbf{W}}
, and
{\mathbf{X}}
satisfying
{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}\subset{\mathbf{X}}
;
{\mathbf{W}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}
share the same inner product
\left(\cdot\middle|\cdot\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
, while
{\mathbf{X}}
has a weaker inner product
\left(\cdot\middle|\cdot\right)_{\mathbf{X}}
.
We use the notation
{\mathbb{A}}^{*}
for the Hilbert adjoint of an operator
{\mathbb{A}}
between two Hilbert spaces.

We use the Lebesgue measure on general finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces; this is defined by identifying the Hilbert space with
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
through any orthonormal basis. The resulting measure is independent of the choice of basis.
By the same type of identification any finite-dimensional Hilbert space admits a ‘standard normal distribution’ and a ‘standard Brownian motion’.
The ‘narrow’ convergence of measures on a Hilbert space
{\mathbf{Y}}
is defined by duality with
{\mathrm{C}}_{b}({\mathbf{Y}})
.

Key assumptions
(see Section
2
for details)

•

{\mathbf{H}}
,
{\mathbf{X}}
, and
{\mathbf{Z}}
are real separable Hilbert spaces, with

\dim{\mathbf{Z}}<\infty
and with compact embedding
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
such that

{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}{\mathbf{X}}
, where
{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}^{*}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
is positive definite and
trace class;

•

{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
is the generator of both a strongly continuous unitary group

\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
, and
\widehat{S}_{t}
is an extension forming a
strongly continuous group on
{\mathbf{X}}
;

•

{\mathbb{P}}
is the orthogonal projection in
{\mathbf{H}}
onto the
finite-dimensional linear subspace
{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
;

•

{\mathsf{C}}
is a bounded linear map from
{\mathbf{Z}}
to
{\mathbf{H}}
satisfying

{\mathbb{P}}{\mathsf{C}}={\mathsf{C}}
;

•

\beta>0
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{2}({\mathbf{Z}};{\mathbb{R}})
is such

\exp\left(-\beta\left({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right)\right)\in L^{1}({\mathbf{Z}}),

and
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is globally Lipschitz continuous;

•

{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
is a skew-adjoint linear operator on
{\mathbf{Z}}
;

•

\gamma_{\beta}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbf{X}}}\left(0,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}\right)
,
\mu_{\beta}=\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathrm{d}}z){\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{X}}(-{\mathsf{C}}z,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}})({\mathrm{d}}\eta)
,
and

\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathrm{d}}z)=\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta}}\exp\!\big({-}\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z){+}\frac{\beta}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\big)\;\!\mathrm{d}z;

(1.13)

•

Compression Property
1.2
:

{\mathbb{P}}\;\!\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}|_{\mathbf{W}}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
for
t\geq 0
; and

{\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*}
has strictly positive eigenvalues.

Data availability statement

No datasets were generated in the preparation of this paper.

Statements and Declarations

The authors have no relevant financial or non-financial interests to disclose.

2
The microscopic system

The microscopic setup of this paper is a well-known model (see,
e.g.,
[
JaP98
,
KS∗02
,
Rey06
]
). As mentioned above, two separate
Hamiltonian systems are coupled, where one is finite-dimensional (‘System A’), and the other is
infinite-dimensional and is interpreted as a heat bath (‘System B’). System A may be nonlinear, while
System B and the coupling will be linear. This is illustrated in Figure
1.1
.

We construct the microscopic system and its properties in the following steps:

1.

We define the two subsystems in spaces
{\mathbf{Z}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}
and their
coupling in state space
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}

(Section
2.1
);

2.

We consider the heat-bath subsystem at positive temperature, which
requires extending the state space
{\mathbf{H}}
to a larger space
{\mathbf{X}}

(Section
2.2
);

3.

We study the evolution of the heat bath on this larger state space, and
characterize the observable part (Lemma
2.4
);

4.

We use the properties of the heat bath to establish existence of
solutions of the coupled system (Theorem
2.5
).

2.1
Finite energy, deterministic evolution

We first recall from the Introduction the microscopic system at finite energy and without
randomness. System A is a fixed Hamiltonian system
({\mathbf{Z}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}})
, where

{\mathbf{Z}}
is a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a nonlinear
Hamiltonian on
{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfying some conditions (see below), and
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}

defines a possibly state-dependent Poisson structure, i.e.
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\colon{\mathbf{Z}}\to{\mathbf{Z}}
is a skew-symmetric linear operator satisfying
\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits_{z}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}=0
and
Jacobi’s identity (
4.2
). We write
z\in{\mathbf{Z}}
for the
variables in System A, and the uncoupled motion of System A is governed by the
equation
\dot{z}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)
.

System B is imagined to be a ‘heat bath’. Concretely, System B is a Hamiltonian
system
({\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
, where
{\mathbf{H}}
is an infinite-dimensional separable
real Hilbert space,
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}:=\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
, and
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
is a
densely defined, unbounded skew-adjoint operator on
{\mathbf{H}}
that generates
a strongly continuous, unitary group
(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. We
write
\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}
for the states of the heat bath, and the uncoupled
evolution of the heat bath is formally given by
\dot{\eta}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\eta={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)
. In particular,
\eta(t)=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\eta(0)
describes
the evolution of the heat bath.

We assume that the heat bath is ‘large’
1
1

1

In the context of this paper, the heat bath being ‘large’
translates into assumptions that the heat bath has many dimensions and a large total energy; in the limit
this becomes an assumption of infinite-dimensionality and a Gaussian measure. We explore this limit in
detail in Section
4.6
.
, but that only part of the heat bath is observable by
System A. This limited observability is implemented by an orthogonal projection operator

{\mathbb{P}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{W}}={\mathbb{P}}{\mathbf{H}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
and its complement
{\mathbb{Q}}:={\mathbb{I}}-{\mathbb{P}}
. System A only
perceives the heat bath through the operator
{\mathbb{P}}
. The range
{\mathbf{W}}
of
{\mathbb{P}}
is assumed to be a
finite-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbf{H}}
, and we use the important Compression Property
1.2
about

{\mathbb{P}}
and
{\mathbf{W}}
that we already mentioned in the introduction. Note that
{\mathbb{P}}
is self-adjoint in
{\mathbf{H}}
.

The coupling between the macroscopic system and the heat bath is given via a linear coupling operator

{\mathsf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{Z}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
defining the bilinear coupling Hamiltonian

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(z,\eta):=\left({\mathbb{A}}z\middle|{\mathbb{P}}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\big(\underbrace{{\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{A}}}_{{\mathsf{C}}}z|\eta\big)_{\mathbf{H}}.

This expression shows how the impact of the heat bath on System A is mediated
by the operator
{\mathbb{P}}
. The total microscopic Hamiltonian system then is the
sum of the three parts,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z,\eta)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)+{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{C}}(z,\eta)

(2.1)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}

(2.2)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\eta{+}{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}.

(2.3)

We can now make the conditions on
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
concrete: we assume that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in C^{2}({\mathbf{Z}})
such that
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is globally Lipschitz continuous,
and that for our chosen
\beta>0
we have

\exp\left(-\beta\left({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right)\right)\in L^{1}({\mathbf{Z}}).

(2.4)

The coupled (finite-energy) microscopic system is defined in terms of the
total Hamiltonian system

({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
, where
the Poisson operator

{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z):={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z){\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\mathbin{\colon}{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
still is skew-adjoint and satisfies Jacobi’s
identity (
4.2
), since
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
is constant. The associated
Hamiltonian equations are (
1.4
), repeated here for convenience

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\big({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z_{t})+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta_{t}\big),

(2.5a)

\displaystyle\dot{\eta}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\,\big(\eta_{t}+{\mathsf{C}}z_{t}\big).

(2.5b)

Here we follow the convention from probability to denote the time-dependence of a variable with a subscript

t
. When necessary for
better readability we write the time dependence in parentheses, as
in
z(s)
. Existence and uniqueness of solutions of these equations is proved
in Theorem
2.5
below. This will need a suitable weak
formulation of (
2.5b
) because
\mathrm{range}({\mathsf{C}})\subset{\mathbf{W}}
is not contained in the domain of the unbounded operator
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
(see Remark
C.2
).

Remark 2.1

(Convention for the derivative
{\mathrm{D}}
)
.

In the context of Hilbert spaces, there is a choice to consider either the Fréchet derivative, which is an element of the dual space, or the ‘gradient’, which is the Riesz representative of the Fréchet derivative and therefore an element of the primal space. In this paper we always choose the second interpretation, as the Riesz representative of the Fréchet derivative.
With this choice, we have for instance for all
\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}
and
z\in{\mathbf{Z}}
,

{\mathrm{D}}_{\eta}\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}=\eta,\qquad{\mathrm{D}}_{\eta}\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}={\mathsf{C}}z,\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathrm{D}}_{z}\left({\mathsf{C}}z\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}={\mathrm{D}}_{z}\left(z\middle|{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta\right)_{\mathbf{Z}}={\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta.

This choice also implies that an operator such as
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
is an unbounded operator from
{\mathbf{H}}
to
{\mathbf{H}}
(and not from
{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
to
{\mathbf{H}}
).

∎

2.2
Positive temperature: The heat bath

We now extend this setup to positive temperature, first focusing on just the
heat bath and its evolution. We recall in Appendix
A

the properties of Gaussian measures that we use.

Let
\beta>0
be interpreted as inverse temperature. Since the heat-bath
Hamiltonian is
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
, a thermodynamic or
statistical-mechanical point of view (see Section
4.6
)
suggests to consider a Gaussian measure with the formal expression

\gamma_{\beta}({\mathrm{d}}\eta)=\exp\left(-\frac{\beta}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right){\mathrm{d}}\eta

(2.6)

on a suitable space containing
{\mathbf{H}}
. This measure should be the natural invariant measure for the
Hamiltonian evolution
\dot{\eta}_{t}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}\eta_{t}
of the isolated heat bath. To define such a Gaussian
measure on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}
, an embedding into a bigger space is required, and
is provided by the Cameron-Martin setting sketched below. A reason is that, unlike in finite dimensions, a
special structure, namely trace-class nature, of the covariance is required. For example, in infinite
dimensions there is no Gaussian measure with the identity as covariance, unlike in finite dimensions.

We briefly recall the Cameron-Martin setting which establishes a meaning for
this expression; see Appendix
A
for more detail. Let
the Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}
be embedded into a larger separable Hilbert
space
{\mathbf{X}}
, in such a way that the identity map
\mathrm{id}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
is
a Hilbert-Schmidt operator. Then

{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathrm{id}\,\mathrm{id}^{*}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
is of trace class, and we
rigorously define
\gamma_{\beta}
as the centered Gaussian measure

\gamma_{\beta}:={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{X}}\left(0,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}\right).

(2.7)

The space
{\mathbf{H}}
can be considered to be the ‘Cameron-Martin space’ of
\gamma_{\beta}
.

From now on we will consider the larger space
{\mathbf{X}}
as the state space for the
heat bath, and the larger space
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
as the state space for the whole
system. We therefore need to extend the operator
{\mathbb{P}}
from
{\mathbf{H}}
to

{\mathbf{X}}
. By Lemma
A.7
, the operator

{\mathbb{P}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
has a
measurable linear extension

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
, which is
\gamma_{\beta}
-a.e. uniquely characterized by the property that it is linear and coincides with

{\mathbb{P}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
(see Definition
A.6
). The
complementary operator
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathrm{id}-\widehat{\mathbb{P}}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}

similarly is a measurable linear extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}
. We also define

\widehat{\mathsf{C}}^{*}:={\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{Z}}
, which is a measurable extension of

{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{Z}}
.

Remark 2.2

(Positive temperature means infinite energy)
.

The properties of Gaussian measures imply that if
\eta\sim\gamma_{\beta}
,
then
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}=+\infty
almost surely—despite
the fact that
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\eta)
appears in the exponent in (
2.6
).
This can be recognized by choosing an orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}

of
{\mathbf{H}}
, and observing that the vector

\big(\left(\eta\middle|e_{1}\right)_{\mathbf{H}},\dots,\left(\eta\middle|e_{n}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big)
of the first
n

coordinates of
\eta
in this basis has variance
n/\beta

(see (
A.16
)); therefore the
expectation of
\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
can not be finite. See,
e.g.,
[
Rey06
, Sec. 2.1]
or
[
Bog98
, Rem. 2.2.3]
for a
discussion of this property.

As a result, ‘finite energy’ requires ‘zero temperature’; positive temperature
implies infinite energy.

∎

We formally denote the extended system with finite temperature (and
infinite energy) by

\mathfrak{S}_{\beta}:=({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}^{\infty},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}},\gamma_{\beta})
, such that the new ingredients

{\mathbf{X}}\supset{\mathbf{H}}
and
\gamma_{\beta}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{X}}(0,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}})
are
visible. Here
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}^{\infty}
coincides with

\cal{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}
on
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
and equals
+\infty
for

\eta\in{\mathbf{X}}\setminus{\mathbf{H}}
. In Section
2.3
we discuss how the
solution operator for the finite-energy microscopic system

({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}},{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}},{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\oplus}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})
can be
extended to the infinite-energy system
\mathfrak{S}_{\beta}
.

The evolution operator
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
, that describes the heat-bath unitary
evolution in the smaller space
{\mathbf{H}}
, can similarly be extended

\gamma_{\beta}
-a.e. to an operator
\widehat{S}_{t}
on

{\mathbf{X}}
. Without loss of generality we make the following simplifying assumption
(see Section
C.2
for a discussion):

Assumption 2.3

(Continuous evolution of the heat bath)
.

The operator
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
can be extended to a strongly
continuous group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
of bounded linear operators
\widehat{S}_{t}

from
{\mathbf{X}}
to
{\mathbf{X}}
.

The extended operators
\widehat{S}_{t}
leave
\gamma_{\beta}
invariant as is
shown in Lemma
A.9
.

For random initial datum
\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}
, the process

t\mapsto\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}

is a stochastic process in
{\mathbf{X}}
; its evolution is deterministic in time, but
the randomness of the initial datum implies that the process as a whole is
stochastic. Under Assumption
2.3
, this process has
continuous sample paths in
{\mathbf{X}}
. The next lemma states some properties of
the
projected
version of this process, which has values in
{\mathbf{W}}
, which
is equipped with the stronger norm of
{\mathbf{H}}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Adopt the setup above, including Assumption
2.3
, and set

Y_{t}(\eta_{0}):=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}\quad\text{for all}\quad t\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}.

Then, the following holds:

1.

For every
\eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{H}}
we have
t\mapsto Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\in C({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
.

2.

Choose the probability space
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}},\gamma_{\beta})
, where
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}
is the
\gamma_{\beta}
-completion of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})
. If
\eta_{0}
is drawn from
{\mathbf{X}}
with law
\gamma_{\beta}
, then both
{Y_{t}}={Y_{t}}(\eta_{0})
and
{Y_{t}}={Y_{t}}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
are measurable
{\mathbf{W}}
-valued stochastic processes with sample paths in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
; in addition
{Y_{t}}(\eta_{0})
is stationary.

The proof is given in Appendix
B
.
Part
1
, the continuity in
{\mathbf{H}}
, follows directly from
the definitions; but more interesting is part
2
,
where we re-interpret the evolution as a stochastic process. The randomness of
this process is naturally parametrized by the starting point
\eta_{0}
drawn
from
\gamma_{\beta}
, leading to the choice of probability space

({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}},\gamma_{\beta})
. The fact that the sample
paths are in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
follows from the fact that for fixed
t
,

Y_{t}(\eta_{0})
is a Gaussian random variable in
{\mathbf{W}}
with finite and

t
-independent variance, so that

{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}}\int_{a}^{b}\|Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{a}^{b}{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}}\|Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=C\;\!(b{-}a)\qquad\text{for }{-}\infty<a<b<\infty.

The additional characterization of
{Y_{t}}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
will be useful below, when we solve the initial-value problem with a given value of
w_{0}:=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}
.

2.3
Well-posedness of the stochastic process on the product space

With the characterization of the process
{Y_{t}}
by Lemma
2.4
we can now construct the solution of the coupled microscopic system on the product space
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
(deterministic and with finite energy) or
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
(random and with infinite energy).

In Theorem
2.5
below we also transform the equation (
2.5b
) into a form that allows for a rigorous definition of a solution. The issue in (
2.5b
) is that the range of the operator
{\mathsf{C}}
might not be included in the domain of the unbounded operator
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
; the integrated versions (
2.8b
) and (
2.9b
) address this.

Theorem 2.5
.

Assume that
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}
is globally Lipschitz continuous on
{\mathbf{Z}}
. Then there exists a collection of nonlinear operators
\{{\mathbb{U}}_{t}\}_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
with the following properties:

1.

For
(z_{0},\eta_{0})\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
, the mapping

t\mapsto(z_{t},\eta_{t}):={\mathbb{U}}_{t}(z_{0},\eta_{0})
has the regularity

z\in C^{1}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{Z}})
and
\eta\in C({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{H}})
, and it satisfies

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z_{t})+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta_{t})

for

\displaystyle t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(2.8a)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}t}\left(\eta_{0}+{\mathsf{C}}z_{0}\right)-{\mathsf{C}}z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\!\mathrm{e}^{{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t-s)}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s

for

\displaystyle t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(2.8b)

2.

For all
z_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\gamma_{\beta}
-a.e.
\eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}
, the function
t\mapsto(z_{t},\eta_{t}):={\mathbb{U}}_{t}(z_{0},\eta_{0})

has the regularity
z\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{Z}})
,

\eta\in C({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{X}})
and it satisfies

\displaystyle\dot{z}_{t}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z_{t})+\widehat{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\eta_{t}),

\displaystyle\text{a.e. }t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(2.9a)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}

\displaystyle=\widehat{S}_{t}\left(\eta_{0}{+}{\mathsf{C}}z_{0}\right)-{\mathsf{C}}z_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\!\mathrm{e}^{{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}(t-s)}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(2.9b)

(note the extended operators
\widehat{\mathsf{C}}^{*}

in (
2.9a
) and
\widehat{S}_{t}

in (
2.9b
)). For fixed
z_{0}
and random

\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}
, the resulting stochastic process

(z_{t},\eta_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a measurable
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
-valued process on the
probability space
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}},\gamma_{\beta})
.

3.

Fix
z_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
and
w_{0}\in{\mathbf{W}}
, and let

\eta_{0}=w_{0}+\xi_{0}
with
\xi_{0}\in\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbf{X}}
and

\xi_{0}\sim\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
. For almost every such
\xi_{0}
the curve

(z_{t},\eta_{t}):={\mathbb{U}}_{t}(z_{0},\eta_{0})
has the same properties as under
part
2
above.

Proof.

By integrating (
2.5b
) and substituting
into (
2.5a
) we find the equation for
z
alone,

\dot{z}_{t}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}Y_{t}+{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}z_{0}{-}{\mathsf{C}}z_{t})\right)+{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s){\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s,

(2.10)

where
{Y_{t}}={Y_{t}}(\eta_{0})
. For
(z_{0},\eta_{0})\in{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}
, the process

t\mapsto Y_{t}(\eta_{0})
is continuous with values in
{\mathbf{W}}
by
Lemma
2.4
, and a standard ODE proof based on contraction for small
times gives short-time existence and uniqueness of a solution of (
2.10
)
with the specified regularity. The global Lipschitz bound on
{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}

implies that the time of existence does not depend on the initial datum, and we
obtain existence globally in time. Equation (
2.8a
)
can then be recovered from (
2.10
) by differentiating in time, and
equation (
2.8b
) can be used to reconstruct

\eta
. This proves the part 1 of the theorem.

For
z_{0}\in{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}
, the stochastic process

Y(\eta_{0})
has sample paths almost surely in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
by
Lemma
2.4
. Again an ODE contraction proof gives the unique
existence of a solution of (
2.10
) with regularity

z\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{Z}})
. To recover
\eta
, remark that

X=X_{t}(\eta_{0}):=\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}
has sample paths in

C({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{X}})
; again equation (
2.8b
) can be
used to define
\eta
in terms of
z
. This proves the existence in
part
2
. The measurability of
(z,\eta)
jointly in
t

and
\eta_{0}
follows from observing that
(t,\eta_{0})\mapsto Y_{t}(\eta_{0})
is
measurable by Lemma
2.4
, and by Fubini the measurability transfers
to the integral in time.

For part
3
, we use that
Y_{t}(\eta)
is linear in

\eta
and split
Y_{t}
in the first term on the
right-hand side in (
2.10
) as

Y_{t}(\eta_{0})=Y_{t}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})+Y_{t}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0})=Y_{t}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})+{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}.

The second term on the right-hand side above equals
{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}w_{0}
and is
continuous in time; the first term again has sample paths almost surely in

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
by Lemma
2.4
. The sum therefore
almost surely is in
L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
and can be treated the same
way as in the proof of part
2
.
∎

Example 2.6

(Running example, part 3)
.

For our model discussed in Examples
1.1

and
1.5
we can explicitly construct the larger Hilbert
space
{\mathbf{X}}\supset{\mathbf{H}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{R}}^{3})={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})^{3}
. In fact, this can be done
independently for each of the three components. We follow the classical
construction in
[
Rey06
]
and choose

{\mathbf{X}}=X{\times}X{\times}X\quad\text{with }X:={\mathrm{H}}^{-2}({\mathbb{R}})+{\mathrm{L}}^{2}_{-2}({\mathbb{R}}).

Here
X
contains distributions
T
that can be decomposed into
T=T_{1}+T_{2}

with
T_{1}\in{\mathrm{H}}^{-2}(R)
and
T_{2}\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2}_{-2}({\mathbb{R}})
, i.e.
x\mapsto(1{+}x^{2})^{-1}T_{2}(x)\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Clearly, the shift operator
\widehat{S}_{t}:T\mapsto T(\!\;\cdot\;\!{-}t)
generates a continuous semigroup that is the
extension of the unitary shift group
(\mathrm{e}^{t\partial_{x}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on

{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
.

The regularity theory for Brownian motion shows that for all
\alpha\in[0,1/2)
the paths
t\mapsto B_{t}
lie in

{\mathrm{H}}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}})
almost surely, but not in
{\mathrm{H}}^{1/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}})
(see e.g.
[
HyV08
]
). Thus, the white-noise distributions
\eta={\mathrm{d}}B_{t}

lie in
H^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}})
only.
Moreover, our dilation functions
f_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
, in
Example
1.5
have a jump at
y=0
and are otherwise
smooth. Hence, they lie in
{\mathrm{H}}^{\beta}({\mathbb{R}})
with
\beta<1/2
, but not in

H^{1/2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Thus, the extension of the projection
{\mathbb{P}}:h\to\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left(f_{j}\middle|h\right)f_{j}
to
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
remains nontrivial in the sense that the subspace

L
of
{\mathbf{X}}
of full measure needs to be constructed by the abstract methods
of Lemma
A.7
.

∎

2.4
Invariant measure on the product space
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}

Since the stochastic process on
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
is a Hamiltonian system with the
Hamiltonian
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}
given in (
2.1
), a
natural stationary measure should be of the (formal) form

\exp\big(-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z,\eta)\big)\;\!\mathrm{d}z\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

We make this expression rigorous by splitting off the
infinite-dimensional Gaussian component, as in (
2.3
):

\exp\left(-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathrm{total}}(z,\eta)\right)=\exp\left(-\beta\big({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\big)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\beta}{2}\|\eta{+}{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right).

This leads to the rigorous definition

\mu_{\beta}({\mathrm{d}}z\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta):=\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathrm{d}}z){\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{X}}(-{\mathsf{C}}z,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}})({\mathrm{d}}\eta),

(2.11)

where

\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathrm{d}}z)=\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta}}\exp\left(-\beta\big({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\big)\right)\;\!\mathrm{d}z.

(2.12)

The following result shows that the measure
\mu_{\beta}
as defined above is
indeed invariant under the flow. The proof needs some nontrivial steps because
of the coupling of the finite-dimensional and the
infinite-dimensional system. On the one hand,
{\mathbf{W}}
is not contained in the
domain of
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and hence not in the domain of the generator of the extended
group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. On the other hand, the measure
\gamma_{\beta}
is
invariant under
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
, but the covariance matrix
{\mathbb{C}}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
is not, see the proof of Lemma
A.9
.

Theorem 2.7

(Invariance of
\mu_{\beta}
)
.

Assume the above setting, including Assumption
1.2
, as well as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbf{Z}};\mathrm{Lin}({\mathbf{Z}}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)=0\text{ on }{\mathbf{Z}},

(2.13)

\displaystyle z\mapsto\big(1+\|{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z){-}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}{\mathsf{C}}z\|\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|^{2}/2)}\quad\text{lies in }{\mathrm{L}}^{1}({\mathbf{Z}}).

(2.14)

Then, the measure

\mu_{\beta}
defined in (
2.11
) is invariant under the flow generated by
(
2.9
).

Proof.

We do a coordinate transformation to simplify the structure of

\mu_{\beta}
as well as of the evolution equation.

By introducing the new variables
(z_{t},\zeta_{t})=\widetilde{M}_{t}(z,\eta)=\big(z,\widehat{S}_{-t}(\eta{+}{\mathsf{C}}z)\big)

the measure
\mu_{\beta}
is transformed into

\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}=(\widetilde{M}_{t})_{\#}\mu_{\beta}=\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathrm{d}}z)\,\gamma_{\beta}(\;\!\mathrm{d}\zeta).

The result is simple for
t=0
because we only shift by the mean value. This
leads to
\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}
. The subsequent transformation by
\widehat{S}_{t}
does not change the
resulting product measure because
\gamma_{\beta}
is invariant (see Lemma
A.9
).

Thus, it remains to show that the measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}
is invariant under the
transformed evolution equation for
(z,\zeta)
. Using the linear
structure of
\widetilde{M}_{t}
we see that
(z_{t},\eta_{t})
is a solution of
(
2.9
) if and only if
(z_{t},\zeta_{t})=\widetilde{M}_{t}(z_{t},\eta_{t})
solves

\dot{z}_{t}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z_{t})\big({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z_{t})+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\zeta_{t}\big),\quad\zeta_{t}=\zeta_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-s{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s,

where
{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z):={\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathsf{C}}z\|^{2}
.
In particular, we conclude that
t\mapsto\zeta_{t}\in{\mathbf{X}}
is differentiable
with a derivative in the smaller space
{\mathbf{H}}
. Hence, we can use that
\zeta_{t}
satisfies the differential equation

\dot{\zeta}_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{t}\in{\mathbf{H}}\quad\text{with initial
condition }\zeta_{0}=\eta_{0}+{\mathsf{C}}z_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}.

The advantage of using
\zeta
is that the unbounded generator
\widehat{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
of the group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
does not appear.

We will show in Lemma
3.2
that
\zeta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}
implies
that
Y_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\zeta_{0}
is an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, such that
the function
t\mapsto Y_{t}
is almost surely continuous. As a consequence one
can show as in Theorem
2.5
that

t\mapsto(z_{t},\zeta_{t}{-}\zeta_{0})
lies in

{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}})
.

Let us denote by
\Phi_{t}\colon{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
with

(z_{t},\zeta_{t})=\Phi_{t}(z_{0},\zeta_{0})
the corresponding flow map. Then,
invariance of
\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}
means
\widehat{\mu}_{\beta}^{(t)}:=(\Phi_{t})_{\#}\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}=\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. The invariance follows from the
transport equation for
t\mapsto\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}^{(t)}
, namely

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\mu}_{\beta}^{(t)}+\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}}\big(V_{t}(z,\zeta)\widehat{\mu}_{\beta}^{(t)}\big)=0,

\displaystyle\text{with }V_{t}(z,\zeta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\,({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z){+}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\zeta)\\[3.00003pt]
\widehat{S}_{-t}\,{\mathsf{C}}\,{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)\,({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z){+}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\zeta)\end{array}\right),

which is to be understood in the sense of distributions by applying suitable
test functions in
{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}})
. (For clarity, we have written

{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
instead of
\widehat{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}}
, which emphasizes that the only
unbounded term is
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
.)

Thus, it remains to show the identity

\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}}\Big\langle\binom{{\mathrm{D}}_{z}\widecheck{\Phi}(z,\zeta)}{{\mathrm{D}}_{\zeta}\widecheck{\Phi}(z,\zeta)},V_{t}(z,\zeta)\Big\rangle\,\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}(\;\!\mathrm{d}z,\;\!\mathrm{d}\zeta)=0\text{ for all }\widecheck{\Phi}\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}).

(2.15)

Indeed, testing with functions

\widecheck{\Phi}(z,\zeta)=\widetilde{\Phi}(z,\widehat{S}_{t}\zeta)
and using the invariance
of
\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta}=\mu_{\beta,{\mathbf{Z}}}\,\gamma_{\beta}
under

\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{Z}}{\oplus}\widehat{S}_{t}
we see that it suffices to prove
(
2.15
) for
t=0
only.

For the final step we work with cylindrical test functions, see

[
AGS05
, Def. 5.1.11]
. For this, we choose the orthogonal basis
generated by
{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}^{*}>0
in
{\mathbf{X}}
, and denote by
{\mathsf{Q}}_{n}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
the
orthogonal projection on to the span of the eigenfunctions associated with the
n

largest eigenvalues. Because
{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}{\mathbf{X}}
we have
{\mathbf{H}}_{n}\coloneq{\mathsf{Q}}_{n}{\mathbf{X}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
and we may use the decomposition
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{H}}_{n}{\otimes}{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}
with

{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}=({\mathbb{I}}{-}{\mathsf{Q}}_{n}){\mathbf{X}}
. Moreover, since the kernel of

{\mathbb{C}}
is trivial, the restriction
{\mathbb{C}}_{n}={\mathbb{C}}|_{{\mathbf{H}}_{n}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}_{n}\to{\mathbf{H}}_{n}
is invertible and we may choose the scalar product of

{\mathbf{H}}
on the finite-dimensional space
{\mathbf{H}}_{n}
.

Writing
\eta=h+y
with
h\in{\mathbf{H}}_{n}
and
y\in{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}
, we now consider
the cylindrical test functions
\widecheck{\Phi}(z,\eta)=\Psi(z,h)
with

\Psi\in{\mathrm{C}}^{1}_{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}_{n})
, i.e. we restrict to a
finite-dimensional space, where computations are classical.

To integrate over the infinite-dimensional space
{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}
we observe that
assumption (
2.14
) implies that the integrand in
(
2.15
) lies in
{\mathrm{L}}^{1}({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}_{n}{\times}{\mathbf{Y}}_{n},\widetilde{\mu}_{\beta})
. For
this we also use that
{\mathbf{X}}\ni\eta\mapsto\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta\in{\mathbf{W}}

lies in
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbf{X}},\gamma_{\beta})
, see
part
2
of
Lemma
A.7
, i.e. equation (
A.16
).

Hence, we can apply Fubini’s
theorem and integrate first over
y\in{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}
. Here we can exploit that the
integrand has the affine form
a_{\Psi}(z,h)+\big(b_{\Psi}(z,h)\big|\widehat{\mathbb{P}}y\big)_{\mathbf{H}}

and that the measure on
{\mathbf{Y}}_{n}
is a centered Gaussian. Thus, the linear term
vanishes, and we are left with an integral over
{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}_{n}
, namely

LHS(
2.15
)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}_{n}}\!\!\Big\langle\binom{{\mathrm{D}}_{z}\Psi(z,h)}{{\mathrm{D}}_{y}\Psi(z,h)},\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z){+}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}h)\\
{\mathsf{C}}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z){+}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}h)\end{array}\right)\!\Big\rangle\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2})}}{\widetilde{Z}_{\beta}}\;\!\mathrm{d}z\;\!\mathrm{d}h,

where we used that
h\in{\mathbf{H}}_{n}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
which implies that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}y={\mathbb{P}}y
and
hence
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}y={\mathsf{C}}^{*}{\mathbb{P}}y={\mathsf{C}}^{*}y
. Moreover, the induced
measure from
\gamma_{\beta}
on the subspace
{\mathbf{H}}_{n}
is
\widehat{Z}_{\beta}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}/2}\;\!\mathrm{d}h
.

Neglecting the irrelevant normalization constant
\widetilde{Z}_{\beta}
and integrating by
parts we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{H}}_{n}}\Psi(z,h)

\displaystyle\big({-}M_{1}(z,h)-M_{2}(z,h)+\beta M_{3}(z,h)\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\beta({\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2})}\;\!\mathrm{d}z\;\!\mathrm{d}h

with

\displaystyle M_{1}(z,h)=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{Z}}}({\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathsf{C}}(z){+}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}h)),
