combination of random heat-bath initial data, Hamiltonian propagation, and
projection generates a noise that is memoryless, leading to the Brownian noise
terms in the equations (
3.3
) and (
4.16
). This is best
expressed by Lemma
3.2
, and the proof of that lemma clearly shows
how the projection and Hamiltonian propagation combine to give the covariance
function of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process.

In addition, the same compression property has a clear connection to the

GENERIC
structure. The operator
{\mathbb{D}}
that appears in the compression
property generates terms in both the Poisson operator
{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
and the
Onsager operator
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
: the skew-symmetic part of
{\mathbb{D}}
contributes to

{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
, and the symmetric part to
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
. In this way the compression
property generates new structure in the (formerly) Hamiltonian system, which
turns out to be
GENERIC
structure.

Finally, the fluctuations generated by the noise are linked to the Onsager
operator
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
by the fluctuation-dissipation
relation (
4.19
). This puts the
GENERIC
framework on a firmer
footing, and gives modelling insight into the various components of
GENERIC
.

5.1
Relation with models and results in the literature

Coupled Hamiltonian systems consisting of a fixed system and a ‘heat bath’ have
been studied by many authors. The earliest example that we could find is by
Zwanzig
[
Zwa73
]
, and similar systems return
in
[
Zwa05
,
Zwa01
,
KS∗02
]
. The system of this paper is a generalization of
that of
[
Zwa73
]
to an infinite-dimensional heat bath. Infinite-dimensional heat baths were also used in the
related work of Jaksic and Pillet
[
JaP98
]
and
Rey-Bellet
[
Rey06
]
.

The fact that a particular tuning of the properties of the heat bath and its
projector can lead to Markovianity of the projected subsystem was observed by
Ford, Kac, and Mazur
[
FKM65
]
, and later used by many
others
[
Zwa73
,
Zwa05
]
. Stuart and
co-workers
[
StW99
,
KS∗02
]
appear to be the first to give a
rigorous convergence statement in the limit of infinite dimensions of the heat
bath (and the Compression Property
1.2
is effectively
equivalent to the choice of the frequencies of the
n
oscillators in these two
papers).

Very similar ideas were also used to explain dissipation in quantum systems
(so-called open quantum systems), in particular in the famous Caldeira-Leggett model

[
CaL83b
,
CaL83a
]
, see also
[
ToH20
]
where the heat
bath is interpreted in terms of a phonon-number representation. The
straightforward usage of
the classical dilation theory leads to so-called quantum-state diffusion, see
e.g.
[
GiP92
]
, which does not fully account for the true nature of
quantum probability. A genuine dilation theory for open quantum system needs
a quantum probability theory, see e.g.
[
AFG84
,
Mey95
]
.

Remark 5.1
.

The setup of Jaksic and Pillet
[
JaP98
]
is similar to ours, but
their condition (H3) effectively excludes the case of this paper. This is due
to the already-remarked fact that
{\mathbf{W}}\cap\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}})=\{0\}
,
which is proved in Remark
C.2
. Jaksic and Pillet find from
their (H3) that their equivalent of the process
Y_{t}
has
C^{1}
dependence on
the initial datum
(z_{0},\eta_{0})
(their Theorem 2.1), while because of the
lack of continuity we only obtain measurability.

∎

The coarse-graining of this paper is closely related to the ‘Mori-Zwanzig’
reduction method
[
Mor65
,
Zwa61
,
Zwa01
,
CHK00
,
Sch22
]
. The first step in this method is the expression of the ‘hidden’
variables in terms of the ‘visible’ variables. In this paper
equation (
2.10
) plays the same role. The integral term characterizes
the changes in
z
that are generated by
z
itself but through the heat bath:

\int_{0}^{t}\underbrace{{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s){\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}}_{=:\,K(t-s)}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s.

The Compression Property (
1.5
) amounts to assuming that there
exists a finite-dimensional subspace
{\mathbf{W}}
with corresponding orthogonal
projection
{\mathbb{P}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
and a dissipative operator
{\mathbb{D}}
on
{\mathbf{W}}

such that

\text{(a)}\qquad{\mathsf{C}}^{*}{\mathbb{P}}={\mathsf{C}}^{*}\qquad\text{and\qquad(b)}\qquad K(t)={\mathsf{C}}^{*}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}{\mathsf{C}}\qquad\text{for }t\geq 0.

(5.1)

Condition (a) means that
{\mathbf{W}}
contains all information necessary to characterize the effect of the heat bath on
z
.
Condition (b), on the other hand, amounts to an assumption that
K
can be written as a finite sum of exponentials (one for each of the coordinates); this is sometimes used as an approximation in numerical algorithms
[
Sch22
]
(see also
[
StS19
]
).

The compression property bypasses the usual solving of the ‘hidden-variables’ equation, which in the case of this paper would involve the orthogonal component
\xi_{t}=\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{t}
. The formal generator of this ‘orthogonal dynamics’ should be either
{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}
or
{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathbb{Q}}
, which both are not well defined and tend to be difficult to study, even formally. In the context of the compression property this dynamics is bypassed, and the effect of the hidden variables on the visible ones is instead fully characterized by (
5.1
).

Mori-Zwanzig reduction schemes come in two forms, depending on whether the split is performed in the state space itself (as in this paper) or in the space of probability measures or ‘ensembles’ on the state space. The latter approach has the advantage of lifting a nonlinear evolution to a linear one, thereby allowing the application of linear theory such as the Mori-Zwanzig reformulation. In the case of this paper, we can apply the simpler, first type, since the nonlinearity is confined to System A.

5.2
Relation between positive and zero temperature

In the zero-temperature limit,
\beta\to\infty
, the positive-temperature equations (
3.3
) and (
4.16
) converge to the corresponding deterministic ODEs (
3.2
) and (
4.15
). This can be recognized in the characterization
\Sigma\Sigma^{*}=\beta^{-1}({\mathbb{D}}+{\mathbb{D}}^{*})
of the noise intensity, which implies that
\Sigma\to 0
as
\beta\to\infty
.

It is a natural question whether the
GENERIC
structure itself converges. The answer is that it does not, at least not in this form. The entropy
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}=\beta e+\text{constant}
becomes singular as
\beta\to\infty
, and the Onsager operator
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
vanishes. The product
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}{\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}
, however, is independent of
\beta
, and this suggests that in order to study this limit it would make sense to rescale by

{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathrm{GEN}}}^{\text{rescaled}}:=\frac{1}{\beta}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{GEN}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}^{\text{rescaled}}:=\beta{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}.

The rescaled objects
{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathrm{GEN}}}^{\text{rescaled}}
and
{\mathbb{K}}_{\mathrm{GEN}}^{\text{rescaled}}
are independent of
\beta
, and therefore trivially converge.

As observed in Section
1.4
, the deterministic
GENERIC
evolution, however, is independent of
\beta
.

5.3
Well-preparedness of the heat bath at time zero

The central construction of the microscopic evolution in this paper is done in part
3
of Theorem
2.5
. There the initial state of the ‘visible’ part of the heat bath
w_{0}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}
is assumed to be given, while the ‘invisible’ part
\xi_{0}=\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0}
is drawn randomly from the corresponding conditioned equilibrium measure
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
of the heat bath. This randomness then generates the randomness in the
SDE
(
3.3
) of the projected system
(z,w)
.

In the philosophical debate about irreversibility and coarse-graining (see
e.g.
[
Rob20
]
) such a setup has been criticized: it amounts to an
assumption that the microscopic state at time
t=0
is rather special. This can
be recognized in the fact that while the initial hidden variables

\xi=\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta
have distribution
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
at time 0,
they
will not
have that same distribution at later or earlier
times. (The distribution
\gamma_{\beta}
is invariant under the heat-bath
evolution, but
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
is not.)

In fact, it turns out that the altered distribution of
\xi
at times different
from zero
does not
change the effect of the coarse-graining. To
illustrate this, consider starting the heat bath in
part
3
of Theorem
2.5
at some time

t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
with distribution
\xi_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
. At time

t=0
the corresponding state equals
\widehat{S}_{-t_{0}}\xi_{t_{0}}
. Note that this
propagated state is distributed according to

(\widehat{S}_{-t_{0}}\widehat{\mathbb{Q}})_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
, and is not completely hidden
(
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{-t_{0}}\xi_{t_{0}}\not=0
). In equation (
2.10
)
describing the evolution of
z
, only the first term changes:

\dot{z}_{t}={\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}Y_{t-t_{0}}(\xi_{t_{0}})+{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\left({\mathrm{D}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{A}}(z)+{\mathsf{C}}^{*}(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}z_{0}-{\mathsf{C}}z_{t})\right)+{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{A}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathsf{C}}^{*}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s){\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathsf{C}}\dot{z}_{s}\;\!\mathrm{d}s.

(5.2)

However, under the compression property by Lemma
2.4
the process

t\mapsto Y_{t-t_{0}}(\xi_{t_{0}})
is again a non-stationary Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
process; in other words the
noise
in this process is stationary, even
though the state at time
t=0
is not in equilibrium. It follows that the noise
generated by the heat bath has the same distribution, also if one starts with a
hidden variable of the form
\widehat{S}_{-t_{0}}\xi_{t_{0}}
, where
\xi_{t_{0}}\sim\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
.

5.4
Other derivations of
GENERIC
by coarse-graining

Öttinger’s coarse-graining of Hamiltonian systems.

The closest in spirit to this paper is the coarse-graining approach by
Öttinger
[
Ött05
, Ch. 6]
. The starting point is again a
Hamiltonian system, and a central role is played by a ‘coarse-graining map’
similar to
\pi_{z,w,e}
in (
1.7
). The two approaches
differ in the definition of the coarse-grained state: while in this paper we
consider
\pi_{z,w,e}(z,\eta)
itself to be the coarse-grained state (up to
renormalization of the infinite energy), in
[
Ött05
, Ch. 6]
the
coarse-grained state is in effect a
distribution

\rho_{z,w,e}
on

{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}
, parametrized by
(z,w,e)
.

It is tempting to consider Öttinger’s distribution
\rho_{z,w,e}
as an
approximation of the conditional distribution of
(z,\eta)
given the value of

\pi_{z,w,e}(z,\eta)
. Unfortunately this approach seems to fail: in simple
examples one can verify that the
actual
conditional distribution of

(z,\eta)
is different from the
\rho_{z,w,e}
postulated in Öttinger’s
approach, sufficiently different to make it unclear why
\rho_{z,w,e}
should
be a good approximation for the purpose of coarse-graining. While there are
clear parallels between the two approaches, at this stage it is not clear to us
whether they should be considered effectively the same or not.

Obtaining
GENERIC
from large deviations.

GENERIC
has also been derived from large-deviation principles of stochastic
processes. In
[
DPZ13
]
, Duong and two of us showed how the

GENERIC
structure of the kinetic Fokker-Planck equation can be recognized in
the large-deviations rate functional of a sequence of ‘more microscopic’
stochastic interacting particle systems. Since a large-deviations principle can
also be interpreted as a form of coarse-graining, this gives an independent
derivation of the
GENERIC
structure, but from a process with stochastic
forcing and deterministic initial data, the opposite of the setting of this
paper. In
[
KL∗18
,
KL∗20
]

the arguments of
[
DPZ13
]
were generalized to a larger
class of systems.

Appendix A
Properties of Gaussian measures

In this appendix we recall a number of properties of Gaussian measures that we use in this paper. We will mostly use the notation and definitions of the book on Gaussian Measures by Bogachev
[
Bog98
]
.

A.1
Basic setup

In
{\mathbb{R}}
, a
centered Gaussian random variable

\eta
with variance
\sigma^{2}
, written as
\eta\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})
, has the probability density

\mu({\mathrm{d}}\eta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right)\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

Note that such a scalar random variable has the equivalent formulation

\text{for all $t\in{\mathbb{R}}$, }\quad{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{e}^{it\eta}\right]=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\sigma^{2}t^{2}}{2}}.

Centered Gaussian random variables on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
have the probability density

\mu({\mathrm{d}}\eta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{n}}\det(\Sigma)}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{T}\Sigma^{-2}\eta\right)\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta,

(A.1)

where
\Sigma
is a symmetric positive definite
n\times n
matrix. We now define the
n
-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}
to be
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with the norm
\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}:=\eta^{T}\Sigma^{-2}\eta
, which implies that the dual norm is
\|f\|_{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}=f^{T}\Sigma^{2}f
. Then the expression above takes the form

\mu({\mathrm{d}}\eta)=\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right)\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta

This leads to the following equivalent characterizations of centered Gaussian measures on any finite-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{H}}
be a finite-dimensional real Hilbert space, let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbf{H}})
, and fix
\beta>0
. The following are equivalent:

\displaystyle\mu({\mathrm{d}}\eta)=\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\beta}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right)\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta;

(A.2)

For all
f\in{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
,
f(\eta)\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(0,\beta^{-1}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}^{2}\big)
;

(A.3)

For all
h\in{\mathbf{H}}
,
\left(h\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(0,\beta^{-1}\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\big)
.

(A.4)

In this case, if
\eta\sim\mu
, then for any bounded linear operator
{\mathbb{O}}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{O}}\eta\middle|\eta\right)_{{\mathbf{H}}}=\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{O}}.

(A.5)

Formulations (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) above can be interpreted as stating that the covariance structure of the measure
\mu
is given by the norms of
{\mathbf{H}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
, as in (
A.1
).
In this paper we need
\eta
to be infinite-dimensional (see for instance Remark
C.2
), while still having covariance structure determined by
{\mathbf{H}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
. The following lemma allows us to construct such Gaussian measures.

The critical step is that we need to consider larger spaces
{\mathbf{X}}
, such that
{\mathbf{H}}
is Hilbert-Schmidt-embedded into
{\mathbf{X}}
; the measure is then defined on
{\mathbf{X}}
, and the random variable
\eta
takes values in
{\mathbf{X}}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{H}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}
be finite- or infinite-dimensional real Hilbert spaces, and let
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbf{X}})
. If the embedding
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
is Hilbert-Schmidt, then there exists a measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbf{X}})
with the properties

For all
f\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
,
f(\eta)\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(0,\beta^{-1}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}^{2}\big)
.

(A.6)

For all
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
,
\left(x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(0,\left(\cov_{\mu}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}}\big)
.

(A.7)

Here
\cov_{\mu}
is a self-adjoint non-negative trace-class operator on
{\mathbf{X}}
. If
f(\eta)=\left(x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}
for some
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
, then
\left(\cov_{\mu}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}}=\beta^{-1}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}^{2}
.

This generalizes the finite-dimensional case, where one can take
{\mathbf{H}}={\mathbf{X}}
and
\cov_{\mu}=\beta^{-1}\mathrm{id}
, and the properties above reduce to those of Lemma
A.1
.

Remark A.3

(Definition of infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures)
.

The usual definition of a Gaussian measure on an infinite-dimensional space
{\mathbf{X}}
is that for each bounded linear form
f
on
{\mathbf{X}}
the projected measure
f_{\#}\mu
is a one-dimensional Gaussian measure (e.g.
[
Bog98
, Def. 2.2.1]
); since
{\mathbf{X}}
is Hilbert, each such
f
is an inner product with an
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
, and this coincides with (
A.7
).

∎

Remark A.4

(Necessity of Hilbert-Schmidt embedding)
.

The formulation of Lemma
A.2
suggests that the embedding
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
needs to be Hilbert-Schmidt for the measure
\mu
to exist, and indeed this is necessary. In particular there is no Gaussian measure on an infinite-dimensional space with the identity operator as covariance.

∎

The construction above concerns centered Gaussian measures. Non-centered measures are constructed by translating by
a\in{\mathbf{X}}
, resulting in a random variable with expectation
a
. We write the corresponding measure as
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathbf{X}}(a,\mathrm{cov})
. Formally, such a measure
\gamma
has the form

\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{cov}^{-1}(\eta-a)\middle|(\eta-a)\right)_{{\mathbf{X}}}}\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

(A.8)

This is the sense in which we construct the ‘canonical’ Gaussian measure with formal structure (
2.6
).

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Writing
\mathrm{id}
for the embedding
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
, the operator
{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathrm{id}\,\mathrm{id}^{*}
is a self-adjoint non-negative trace-class operator; by
[
Bog98
, Th. 2.3.1]
there exists a Gaussian measure
\mu
with mean zero and covariance operator
\cov_{\mu}=\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}
, which therefore satisfies (
A.7
) by the definition of a Gaussian measure.

From the definition of
\mathrm{id}:{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
we have
\left(x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}=\left(\mathrm{id}^{*}x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
. For
f\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
of the form
f(\eta)=\left(x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}=\left(\mathrm{id}^{*}x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
for some
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
, we then calculate

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}}\frac{f(\eta)}{\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}}=\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbf{H}}}\frac{\left(\mathrm{id}^{*}x\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}{\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}}=\left(\mathrm{id}^{*}x\middle|\mathrm{id}^{*}x\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\left({\mathbb{C}}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}},

by which also (
A.6
) follows from (
A.7
).
∎

A.2
Construction of
{\mathbf{X}}
by Gelfand triple

In Section
2.2
we aim to construct a Gaussian measure of the formal structure

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}}\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta.

(A.9)

This expression can be made rigorous as a Gaussian measure on a larger Hilbert space
{\mathbf{X}}
with the property that
{\mathbf{H}}
admits a Hilbert-Schmidt embedding into
{\mathbf{X}}
. While essentially ‘any’ such embedding is possible (see
[
Bog98
, Lemma 3.2.2]
), we choose a particular one based on the well-known Gelfand triple. We now describe this setting, which is visualized Fig.
A.1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{H}}\simeq{\mathbf{H}}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}}}
definition

Figure A.1:
The Gelfand-triple setup. The top row is the classical Gelfand triple
{\mathbf{V}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{H}}\simeq{\mathbf{H}}^{*}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
, and the bottom row connects this triple to the notation of this paper. The space
{\mathbf{X}}
is defined as
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
, and
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
is defined as the space of bounded linear maps on
{\mathbf{X}}
. The two dotted lines indicate the two Riesz identifications that would be possible in this diagram; because of the risk of confusion we make these identifications explicitly, not implicitly (see (
A.11
) below).

The space
{\mathbf{X}}
is implicitly characterized by the choice of an unbounded, densely defined, self-adjoint linear operator
{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})\subset{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
with the property that
{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})
is well-defined and Hilbert-Schmidt. Given such an operator we construct
{\mathbf{X}}
in the following steps.

1.

We define
{\mathbf{V}}:=\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})
, which is a Hilbert space with norm
\|v\|_{\mathbb{V}}:=\|{\mathbb{A}}v\|_{\mathbf{H}}
. This implies that
{\mathbf{V}}={\mathbb{A}}^{-1}{\mathbf{H}}
.

2.

We identify
{\mathbf{H}}^{*}
with
{\mathbf{H}}
and define the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
by

\frac{1}{2}\|h\|_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}^{2}:=\sup_{v\in{\mathbf{V}}}\left(h\middle|v\right)_{\mathbf{H}}-\frac{1}{2}\|v\|_{\mathbf{V}}^{2}\qquad\text{for }h\in{\mathbf{H}}.

We then define
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
as the closure of
{\mathbf{H}}
in the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}
. By construction the elements of
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
are in duality with
{\mathbf{V}}
, and we write
{}_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}\!\left\langle\xi,v\right\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}
for the dual product. We then also have
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
, in the sense that any
h\in{\mathbf{H}}
is a bounded linear form on
{\mathbf{V}}
by

{}_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}\!\left\langle h,v\right\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}:=\left(h\middle|v\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\qquad\text{for all }v\in{\mathbf{V}}.

3.

We extend
{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}
as an operator
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\to{\mathbf{H}}
by setting for any
\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}^{*}

\left({\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\xi\middle|h\right)_{\mathbf{H}}:={}_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}\!\left\langle\xi,{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}h\right\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}\qquad\text{for all }h\in{\mathbf{H}}.

We can then write

\|\xi\|_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}=\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\xi\|_{\mathbf{H}}\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in{\mathbf{V}}^{*}.

(A.10)

The extension is also a symmetric operator in
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
, since for
h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathbf{H}}
we have

\left({\mathbb{A}}^{-1}h_{1}\middle|h_{2}\right)_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}=\left(h_{1}\middle|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}h_{2}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\left(h_{1}\middle|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}h_{2}\right)_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}.

We similarly extend
{\mathbb{A}}
as an operator
{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
.

4.

We define
{\mathbf{X}}:={\mathbf{V}}^{*}
. The space
{\mathbf{X}}
is a Hilbert space with norm
\|\xi\|_{\mathbf{X}}=\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\xi\|_{\mathbf{H}}
for all
\xi\in{\mathbf{X}}
.
We have
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
as mentioned above, with
\|h\|_{{\mathbf{X}}}=\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}h\|_{\mathbf{H}}
; this shows that
the embedding
{\mathbf{H}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}
is Hilbert-Schmidt.

5.

We characterize elements of the dual space

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}:=\left\{f\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\text{ linear and bounded }\right\}

in two different ways, by applying Riesz representation on either
{\mathbf{X}}
or
{\mathbf{H}}
, leading to an element
x_{f}\in{\mathbf{X}}
or equivalently an element
v_{f}\in{\mathbf{V}}
:

\displaystyle f(\xi)

\displaystyle=\left(\xi\middle|x_{f}\right)_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in{\mathbf{X}},

(A.11a)

\displaystyle={}_{{\mathbf{V}}^{*}}\!\left\langle\xi,v_{f}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for all }\xi\in{\mathbf{X}},

(A.11b)

\displaystyle=\left(\xi\middle|v_{f}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever }\xi\in{\mathbf{H}}.

(A.11c)

We have
x_{f}={\mathbb{A}}^{2}v_{f}
and
\|f\|_{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}}=\|x_{f}\|_{\mathbf{X}}=\|v_{f}\|_{\mathbf{V}}
.

The setup above defines the space
{\mathbf{X}}
. In order to define the measure (
A.9
) we define the trace-class operator

{\mathbb{C}}:={\mathbb{A}}^{-2}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}\qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad\left({\mathbb{C}}\xi\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{X}}:=\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\xi\|_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}\text{ for }\xi\in{\mathbf{X}}.

We then set, for
\beta>0
,
\gamma_{\beta}
to be the Gaussian measure on
{\mathbf{X}}

\gamma_{\beta}={\mathcal{N}}_{{\mathbf{X}}}(0,\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}).

This can be considered a rigorous version of (
A.9
), because from
\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{X}}=\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}
we deduce that

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\|{\mathbb{A}}\eta\|_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\left({\mathbb{A}}^{2}\eta\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}^{-1}\eta\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{X}}},

which matches with (
A.8
).

Note that
{\mathbb{C}}
also is trace-class as a map
{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
, since in terms of an orthonormal basis
e_{k}
of
{\mathbf{H}}
we can write

\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{C}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{C}}e_{k}\middle|e_{k}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}e_{k}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2},

and
{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}
is Hilbert-Schmidt on
{\mathbf{H}}
.

Remark A.5

(Positive temperature implies infinite energy)
.

A well-known property of infinite-dimensional Gaussian measures such as
\gamma_{\beta}
is that for any
\beta>0
,
\gamma_{\beta}({\mathbf{H}})=0
. This shows that if
\eta
is distributed according to
\gamma_{\beta}
, then the natural energy
\frac{1}{2}\|\eta\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}
is almost surely infinite.
See e.g.
[
Rey06
, Sec. 2.1]
or
[
Bog98
, Rem. 2.2.3]
for a discussion of this property.

∎

To facilitate applying the theorems of
[
Bog98
]
we make explicit the relationship between the objects of this paper and the notation of
[
Bog98
]
.
The covariance bilinear form
R_{\gamma_{\beta}}
is given by

R_{\gamma_{\beta}}(f)(f)=\frac{1}{\beta}\left({\mathbb{C}}x_{f}\middle|x_{f}\right)_{\mathbf{X}}\qquad\text{for $f\in{\mathbf{X}}^{*}$ and for $x_{f}$ given by~\eqref{eq:identification-X*-xf}},

(A.12)

and the Cameron-Martin Hilbert space
H(\gamma_{\beta})
coincides with
{\mathbf{H}}
, up to a scaling of the norm:

\|h\|_{H(\gamma_{\beta})}^{2}=\beta\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }h\in{\mathbf{H}}.

(A.13)

A.3
Measurable extensions of linear maps

In Section
2
we constructed several extensions of linear operators on
{\mathbf{H}}
to
{\mathbf{X}}
. In this section we give the details of these extensions.

Definition A.6

(Measurable linear extensions
of
[
Bog98
, Def. 2.10.1+3.7.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathbf{Y}}
be a normed linear space, and let
f\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{Y}}
be given. If there
exists a subspace
{\mathbf{L}}
with

{\mathbf{H}}\subset{\mathbf{L}}\subset{\mathbf{X}}
and
\gamma_{\beta}({\mathbf{L}})=1
and a
linear map
\widehat{f}\colon{\mathbf{L}}\to{\mathbf{Y}}
, measurable with respect to

({\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{Y}}))
, such that
\widehat{f}
coincides with

f
on
{\mathbf{L}}
, then we call
\widehat{f}
a
measurable linear extension
of
f
.

Such an extension is characterized
{\gamma_{\beta}}
-a.e. by its values on

{\mathbf{H}}

[
Bog98
, Cor. 2.10.8]
, and therefore can be considered

\gamma_{\beta}
-unique.

Note that if
\eta\sim\gamma_{\beta}
and
g
is a measurable map from

{\mathbf{X}}
to
{\mathbf{Y}}
, then
g(\eta)
has distribution
g_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
, the
push-forward measure of
\gamma_{\beta}
under
g
, defined by

\int_{\mathbf{Y}}\varphi(\xi)(g_{\#}\gamma_{\beta})({\mathrm{d}}\xi)=\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi(g(\eta))\gamma_{\beta}({\mathrm{d}}\eta)\qquad\text{for all }\varphi\in C_{b}({\mathbf{Y}}).

Lemma A.7

(Extensions of bounded linear operators on
{\mathbf{H}}
)
.

Let
\eta
have distribution
\gamma_{\beta}
.

1.

If
h\in{\mathbf{H}}
, then the bounded linear map
f_{h}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\ni\eta\mapsto\left(h\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\in{\mathbb{R}}

can be extended to a measurable linear map
\widehat{f}_{h}
, and we have for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathbf{H}}

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\widehat{f}_{h_{1}}(\eta)\widehat{f}_{h_{2}}(\eta)\right]=\mathchoice{{\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{``}\hbox{$\displaystyle\;{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left(h_{1}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\left(h_{2}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big]$}\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{''}}}{{\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{``}\hbox{$\textstyle\;{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left(h_{1}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\left(h_{2}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big]$}\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{''}}}{{\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{``}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left(h_{1}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\left(h_{2}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big]$}\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{''}}}{{\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{``}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\;{\mathbb{E}}\big[\left(h_{1}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\left(h_{2}\middle|\eta\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big]$}\raise 2.05556pt\hbox{''}}}=\frac{1}{\beta}\left(h_{1}\middle|h_{2}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}.\qquad

(A.14)

2.

If
{\mathbb{O}}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
is a bounded linear operator on
{\mathbf{H}}
, then there exists a measurable extension
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}
. Then
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta
is an
{\mathbf{X}}
-valued Gaussian random variable with covariance
\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{O}}
defined by

\frac{1}{\beta}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{\mathbb{O}}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}}:=\frac{1}{\beta}\|{\mathbb{O}}^{*}{\mathbb{C}}x\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2},\qquad x\in{\mathbf{X}},

(A.15)

where
{\mathbb{O}}^{*}
is the adjoint of
{\mathbb{O}}
in
{\mathbf{H}}
.

If in addition
{\mathbb{O}}
has finite rank, then
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta
is an
{\mathbf{H}}
-valued Gaussian random variable with covariance
\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{O}}{\mathbb{O}}^{*}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
. In particular,
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta

has finite
{\mathbf{H}}
-variance given by

{\mathbb{E}}\big\lVert\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta\big\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\beta}\trace_{\mathbf{H}}({\mathbb{O}}{\mathbb{O}}^{*}).

(A.16)

3.

Let
{\mathbb{P}}
be an orthogonal projection on
{\mathbf{H}}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}:=I-{\mathbb{P}}
the complementary projection. Let
X\sim\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
and
Y\sim\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
be independent random variables. Then
X+Y
is distributed according to
\gamma_{\beta}
.

In addition, in order to recognize a probability measure
\zeta\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbf{X}})
as
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
it is sufficient that there exists a subspace
\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}\subset{\mathbf{V}}
, dense in
{\mathbf{H}}
in the
{\mathbf{H}}
-norm, such that for all
h\in\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}

\int_{{\mathbf{X}}}\mathrm{e}^{i\left(h\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}\zeta({\mathrm{d}}\xi)=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\beta}\|{\mathbb{Q}}h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right).

(A.17)

Remark A.8
.

Part
2
above implies in particular that
if
{\mathbb{O}}
is a unitary operator on
{\mathbf{H}}
, then
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta
has the same
covariance as
\eta
.

∎

Proof.

Part
1

is
[
Bog98
, Th. 2.10.11]
in combination with (
A.13
).
In part
2
, the general case
is
[
Bog98
, Th. 3.7.6]
. To calculate the covariance, first note that
since measurable linear extensions are characterized by their values on

{\mathbf{H}}
, the two linear extensions
\widehat{f}_{1}
and
\widehat{f}_{2}
of

f_{1}(\xi):=\left(h\middle|\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad f_{2}(\xi):=\left({\mathbb{O}}^{*}h\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}

are
\gamma_{\beta}
-a.e. equal. It follows that

{\mathbb{E}}|f_{1}(\eta)|^{2}={\mathbb{E}}|f_{2}(\eta)|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:formula-for-covariance}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\beta}\|{\mathbb{O}}^{*}h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}.

The characterization (
A.15
) follows by remarking that for

x\in{\mathbf{X}}
,
\left(x\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{X}}=\left({\mathbb{C}}x\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}
, so that
f_{1}
equals

\left(x\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{X}}
whenever
h={\mathbb{C}}x
.

The case of finite rank follows from
part
1
, by choosing orthonormal bases

\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
and
\{f_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
{\mathbf{H}}
such that that

\mathrm{Range}({\mathbb{O}})\subset\Span\left(\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d}\right)
and

\mathrm{Ker}({\mathbb{O}})^{\perp}\subset\Span\left(\{f_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d}\right)
, and expanding the
squared norm (see also
[
Bog98
, Ex. 3.7.14]
).

In part
3
the distribution of
X+Y
is given by
[
Bog98
, Lemma 3.11.25]
. The characterization
\zeta=\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
can be obtained as follows. By
[
Bog98
, Cor. A.3.13]
, the identity (
A.17
) for all elements of
\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}
uniquely identifies
\zeta
as a centered Gaussian measure on
{\mathbf{X}}
. Writing for
h={\mathbb{C}}x
the exponent as

\left(h\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\left({\mathbb{C}}x\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=\left(x\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{X}},

we observe that
\zeta
has covariance operator

\frac{1}{\beta}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{\zeta}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}}:=\frac{1}{\beta}\|{\mathbb{Q}}h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\beta}\|{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{C}}x\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}.

Using part
2
we recognize this Gaussian measure as the law of
\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta
where
\eta\sim\gamma_{\beta}
.
∎

An important consequence of the above extension results is that the
extended group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
{\mathbf{X}}
preserves the measure

\gamma_{\beta}
.

Lemma A.9

(Invariance of the Gaussian measure)
.

The extension
\widehat{S}_{t}:{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
of
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
(see
Assumption
2.3
) leaves the Gaussian measure

\gamma_{\beta}
invariant.

Proof.

We apply Part
2
of
Lemma
A.7
with the unitary operator

{\mathbb{O}}=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
and its extension
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}=\widehat{S}_{t}
. Hence, starting from

\eta\sim\gamma_{\beta}
we see that the random variable
\widehat{\mathbb{O}}\eta=\widehat{S}_{t}\eta
is a centered Gaussian in
{\mathbf{X}}
with covariance
\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}_{t}

characterized by

\frac{1}{\beta}\left({\mathbb{C}}_{t}x\middle|x\right)_{\mathbf{X}}=\frac{1}{\beta}\|\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{C}}x\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\beta}\|{\mathbb{C}}x\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}.

In particular, this means
\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}_{t}=\beta^{-1}{\mathbb{C}}
; hence

\widehat{S}_{t}\eta
has the same distribution
\gamma_{\beta}
.
∎

Remark A.10

(Invariance of
\gamma_{\beta}
via inner products)
.

The invariance of
\gamma_{\beta}
can also be shown by working in
{\mathbf{X}}
rather
than in
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
as in the above proof. We do not have the commutation property

{\mathbb{C}}\widehat{S}_{t}=\widehat{S}_{t}{\mathbb{C}}
but we have the invariance property

\widehat{S}_{t}^{*}{\mathbb{C}}\widehat{S}_{t}={\mathbb{C}},

(A.18)

which implies
\left({\mathbb{C}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta\middle|\widehat{S}_{t}\eta\right)=\left(\widehat{S}_{t}^{*}{\mathbb{C}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta\middle|\eta\right)=\left({\mathbb{C}}\eta\middle|\eta\right)
.

To see the validity of (
A.18
), we need to calculate the
Hilbert-space adjoint
\widehat{S}_{t}^{*}
of
\widehat{S}_{t}
. For an operator
A\colon{\mathbf{U}}\to{\mathbf{V}}
denote the Banach-space adjoint by
A^{\top}\colon{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\to{\mathbf{U}}^{*}
, which is
defined via
\langle A^{\top}\nu,u\rangle_{\mathbf{U}}=\langle\nu,Au\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}
.
Using the dual space
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
satisfying

{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\subset{\mathbf{H}}\subset{\mathbf{X}}
and the duality isomorphism
{\mathbb{C}}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
we have the relation
\widehat{S}_{t}^{*}={\mathbb{C}}\,\widehat{S}_{t}^{\top}\,{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}
,
where
\widehat{S}_{t}^{\top}\colon{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
.
As
\widehat{S}_{t}
is the extension of
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
from
{\mathbf{H}}
to
{\mathbf{X}}
, the
Banach-space adjoint is simply the restriction

\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}|_{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}}
. Together with
(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})^{\top}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
in

{\mathbf{H}}
, we found the representation

\widehat{S}_{t}^{*}={\mathbb{C}}\,\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}|_{{\mathbf{X}}^{*}}\,{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}
, and the desired
identity (
A.18
) follows.

∎

Appendix B
Delayed proofs

B.1
Section
2
: Microscopic setup

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

Part
1
follows from the assumption that
\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
is
a strongly continuous group on
{\mathbf{H}}
and from the fact that on
{\mathbf{H}}
the operator

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
reduces to the bounded linear operator
{\mathbb{P}}
.

For part
2
, first note that
Y(\eta_{0})
is stationary
since
\gamma_{\beta}
is invariant under
\widehat{S}_{t}
. We next show that both
processes are measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebra

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}){\times}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}
. The following decomposition shows
that
Y_{t}(\eta_{0})
is a measurable function of
(t,\eta_{0})
, with each arrow a
measurable map between the two
\sigma
-algebras:

\displaystyle(t,\eta_{0})\qquad

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}\quad

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}){\times}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{W}})

The first arrow is measurable by the continuity given by
Assumption
2.3
, and the second by the measurability of

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
as a map from
({\mathbf{X}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}})
to

({\mathbf{W}},{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{W}}))
(Lemma
A.7
).

To show that
Y=Y(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
similarly is measurable we decompose it as

Y_{t}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0}=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}-\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}.

We just showed that the first term is measurable from

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}){\times}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}
to
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{W}})
. The
measurability of the second follows from remarking that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}={\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}
,
since the range of
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
is included in
{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
. With this we then have

\displaystyle(t,\eta_{0})\qquad

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle(t,\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0})\qquad

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\quad

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\eta_{0}.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}){\times}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{X}})_{\gamma_{\beta}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbb{R}}){\times}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{W}})

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{H}})\

\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{B}}({\mathbf{W}})\

Here the first arrow is measurable again by the construction of
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}

(Lemma
A.7
), the second by the strong continuity of

\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
, and the third by the continuity of
{\mathbb{P}}
on
{\mathbf{H}}
.

Finally, to show that
Y(\eta_{0})
and
Y(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
have sample paths in

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, we calculate for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
that

{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}}\|Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}}\big\lVert\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\eta_{0}\big\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:variance-of-finite-rank-operator}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\beta}\trace_{\mathbf{H}}\big({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\left({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}\right)^{*}\big)=\frac{1}{\beta}\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{P}}=\frac{\dim{\mathbf{W}}}{\beta}.

The measurability allows us to apply Fubini and calculate for

-\infty<a<b<\infty

{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}}\int_{a}^{b}\|Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\;\!\mathrm{d}t=\int_{a}^{b}{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}}\|Y_{t}(\eta_{0})\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=(b{-}a)\!\;\frac{\dim{\mathbf{W}}}{\beta}.

This shows that almost surely
Y(\eta_{0})
is an element of

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
.

To show the analogous result for
Y(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
, we similarly obtain

{\mathbb{E}}_{\eta_{0}\sim\gamma_{\beta}}\int_{a}^{b}\big\lVert Y_{t}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})\big\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\;\!\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{\beta}(b{-}a)\trace_{\mathbf{H}}({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}}).

To estimate the trace we choose an orthonormal basis
(e_{k})_{k=1}^{d}
of
{\mathbf{W}}

of size
d=\dim{\mathbf{W}}
, and calculate

\displaystyle\trace_{\mathbf{H}}({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left\lvert\left({\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}}e_{k}\middle|e_{k}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left\lVert{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathbb{P}}e_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}\left\lVert e_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left\lVert e_{k}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=d.

Again it follows that
Y(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}\eta_{0})
is almost surely in

L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
.
∎

B.2
Microcanonical measures and Section
3.2

In the physics literature it is common to define ‘microcanonical ensembles’, which effectively are measures on the set of possible states. A useful tool for this is the following delta-function-like object.

Let
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be continuous.
For
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
the non-negative measure
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})

\mu({\mathrm{d}}x):=\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)

is defined by the property

\mu(A)=\lim_{h\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{A}\mathds{1}\big\{a\leq f(x)<a+h\big\}\;\!\mathrm{d}x\in[0,\infty]\qquad\text{for all }A\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

whenever this definition makes sense.

One way of making this definition rigorous is by integration against test functions:

Definition B.1

(
[
LRS10
, (1.25)]
)
.

The measure
\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is defined by the property that for all
F\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
G\in C({\mathbb{R}})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(a)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}F(x)\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\;\!\mathrm{d}a=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}G(f(x))F(x)\;\!\mathrm{d}x.

(B.1)

The right-hand side above is finite for all
F\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
G\in C({\mathbb{R}})
,
since
\supp F
is compact and
G\circ f
therefore is bounded on this
support. This implies that
\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\;\!\mathrm{d}a
can be
considered a locally finite non-negative measure on
(x,a)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}
. For
almost all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
the disintegration
\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)
is
then a locally finite non-negative measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

By the co-area formula (see, e.g.,
[
LRS10
, Sec. 3.2.1]
) this measure can also be expressed with help of the
(n{-}1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}
on the set
S_{a}:=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:f(x)=a\}
as

\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)=\frac{1}{|\nabla f(x)|}{\mathcal{H}}^{n-1}\big|_{S_{a}}({\mathrm{d}}x).

(B.2)

In Section
4.6
we made use of the following property,
which allows us to write a microcanonical measure on a product space as the
product of two such measures on the individual spaces. The crucial element is
the fact that the ‘energy’ function has the additive structure

(x,y)\mapsto f(x)+g(y)
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite-dimensional spaces, and let
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
g\colon Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
be continuous.
Set

T\colon X{\times}Y\to X{\times}Y{\times}{\mathbb{R}},\qquad(x,y)\mapsto(x,y,e:=g(y)).

For Lebesgue almost all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
we then have

\Big(T_{\#}\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+g(y)-a\right]\Big)({\mathrm{d}}x\;\!\mathrm{d}y\;\!\mathrm{d}e)=\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a+e\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\bm{\updelta}\left[g(y)-e\right]({\mathrm{d}}y)\;\!\mathrm{d}e.

Proof.

By the definition of the push-forward
T_{\#}\bm{\updelta}
we need to show that for all
\varphi\in C_{c}(X{\times}Y{\times}{\mathbb{R}})
we have for almost all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}

\int_{X{\times}Y}\varphi(x,y,g(y))\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+g(y)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x\;\!\mathrm{d}y)\\
=\int_{X{\times}Y{\times}{\mathbb{R}}}\varphi(x,y,e)\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)-a+e\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\bm{\updelta}\left[g(y)-e\right]({\mathrm{d}}y)\;\!\mathrm{d}e.

Since in the right-hand integral we can replace
\varphi(x,y,e)
by
\varphi(x,y,g(y))
, it is sufficient to show that for almost all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and for all measurable
A\subset X
,
B\subset Y

\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+g(y)-a\right](A{\times}B)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+e-a\right](A)\bm{\updelta}\left[g(y)-e\right](B)\;\!\mathrm{d}e.

(B.3)

To prove this identity we take
G\in C({\mathbb{R}})
and
F\in C_{c}(X{\times}Y)
and rewrite the left-hand side of (
B.3
) using (
B.1
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(a)\int_{X{\times}Y}F(x,y)\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+g(y)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x\;\!\mathrm{d}y)\;\!\mathrm{d}a

\displaystyle\kern-17.58688pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\eqref{eqdef:pdelta}}}\int_{X{\times}Y}G(\underbrace{f(x)+g(y)}_{=:g_{x}(y)})F(x,y)\;\!\mathrm{d}y\;\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\kern-17.58688pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\eqref{eqdef:pdelta}}}\int_{X}\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(\tilde{e})\int_{Y}F(x,y)\bm{\updelta}\left[g_{x}(y)-\tilde{e}\right]({\mathrm{d}}y)\;\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{e}\;\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\kern-14.90927pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\tilde{e}=e+f(x)}}\int_{X}\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(e+f(x))\underbrace{\int_{Y}F(x,y)\bm{\updelta}\left[g(y)-e\right]({\mathrm{d}}y)}_{=:\tilde{F}_{e}(x)}\;\;\!\mathrm{d}e\;\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{X}G(e+f(x))\tilde{F}_{e}(x)\;\!\mathrm{d}x\;\!\mathrm{d}e

\displaystyle\kern-17.58688pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\eqref{eqdef:pdelta}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(a)\int_{X}\tilde{F}_{e}(x)\bm{\updelta}\left[e+f(x)-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\;\!\mathrm{d}a\;\!\mathrm{d}e

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}G(a)\int_{X{\times}Y{\times}{\mathbb{R}}}F(x,y)\bm{\updelta}\left[f(x)+e-a\right]({\mathrm{d}}x)\bm{\updelta}\left[g(y)-e\right]({\mathrm{d}}y)\;\!\mathrm{d}e\;\;\!\mathrm{d}a.

It follows that for almost all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
the identity (
B.3
) holds.
∎

For the next two lemmas we adopt the setting of Section
4.6
. Since the aim is to illustrate the interpretation of the formula
{\mathcal{S}}=\beta e
, we simplify by assuming that
{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{V}}
. We then can choose an orthonormal basis
\{e_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
of both
{\mathbf{W}}
and
{\mathbf{H}}
consisting of elements of
{\mathbf{V}}
(see
[
Bog98
, Cor. 2.10.10]
). The benefit of choosing the basis in
{\mathbf{V}}
is that the basis vectors can be interpreted as elements of
{\mathbf{X}}^{*}
, allowing us to apply existing density results.

Letting
{\mathbf{W}}
be
\Span\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{d}
, we then construct an increasing sequence of
n
-dimensional subspaces
\Xi_{n}
of the complement
{\mathbf{W}}^{\perp}
,

\Xi_{n}:=\Span\{e_{k}\}_{k=d+1}^{d+n}.

We then define the microcanonical probability measure of radius
\sqrt{R}
:

\nu_{n,R}({\mathrm{d}}\xi):=\frac{1}{Z_{n,R}}\bm{\updelta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}-\frac{nR}{2}\right]\bigg|_{\Xi_{n}}({\mathrm{d}}\xi).

(B.4)

This measure is a normalized version of the measure
\zeta_{n,\beta,e}
in (
4.26
) for the choice
R=\beta^{-1}+2e/n
.

The first result is the ‘equivalence of ensembles’, also known as ‘Boltzmann’s equivalence principle’ or ‘Poincaré’s Lemma’ (see
[
DiF87
, Sec. 6]
for a discussion of the history). This result states that if
X_{n}
is distributed according to
\nu_{n,R}
, then the first
k
coordinates resemble a Gaussian random variable in the limit
n\to\infty
:

Lemma B.3

(Equivalence of ensembles; e.g.
[
DiF87
]
)
.

Let
\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
be an orthonormal basis of
{\mathbf{H}}
such that
\{e_{k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
is a basis of
\Xi_{n}
.
Let
R_{n}\to R>0
and let
X_{n}
have law
\nu_{n,R_{n}}
. Fix
k\geq 1
. As
n\to\infty
, the first
k
coordinates

\big(\left(X_{n}\middle|e_{1}\right)_{\mathbf{H}},\dots,\left(X_{n}\middle|e_{k}\right)_{\mathbf{H}}\big)

converge in distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable in
{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
with covariance
R\,I_{k}
.

The second result is a variance bound for
\nu_{n,R}
, which probably is standard, but for which we could not find a convenient reference. Note that it does not follow from equivalence-of-ensemble results such as Lemma
B.3
since it concerns the variance of the full random variable, not a finite-dimensional marginal.

Lemma B.4

(Variance bound for microcanonical measures)
.

Let
Y_{n}
have distribution
\nu_{n,R}
, and let
{\mathbb{T}}
be a symmetric non-negative trace-class operator on
{\mathbf{H}}
.
Then there exists
C>0
, independent of
R
and
{\mathbb{T}}
, such that

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{T}}^{1/2}Y_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\leq CR\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{T}}\qquad\text{for all }n\geq 1.

In particular,

{\mathbb{E}}\|Y_{n}\|_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}Y_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\leq CR\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{C}}\qquad\text{for all }n\geq 1.

Proof.

Let
X_{n}
be a standard normal random variable in
\Xi_{n}
; then
Y_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{dist}}}{{=}}\sqrt{R}\,X_{n}/\|X_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}
, and therefore

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{T}}^{1/2}Y_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}=R\;{\mathbb{E}}\frac{\|{\mathbb{T}}^{1/2}X_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}}{\|X_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}}.

The random variable
\|X_{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}
is
\chi^{2}(n)
-distributed, and therefore
\|X_{n}\|^{2}_{\mathbf{H}}/n
concentrates onto
1
; it follows that there exists
r_{n}\to 0
such that

{\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{T}}^{1/2}Y_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\leq R(1+r_{n}){\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{T}}^{1/2}X_{n}\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:finite-dimensional-trace}}}{{=}}R(1+r_{n})\trace_{\Xi_{n}}{\mathbb{T}},

where
\trace_{\Xi_{n}}{\mathbb{T}}=\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathcal{P}}_{n}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
in terms of the orthogonal projection
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
in
{\mathbf{H}}
onto
\Xi_{n}
. Since
\trace_{\Xi_{n}}{\mathbb{T}}\leq\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{T}}
, the result follows.
∎

We conclude by giving the proofs of Lemmas
4.13
and
4.14
.

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

To prove part
1
, remark that
\supp\zeta_{n,\beta,e}
is an
n
-dimensional sphere of radius
r=\sqrt{2e+n/\beta}
; using the co-area formula (
B.2
) we find

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,n,e}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Xi_{n}}\bm{\updelta}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\xi\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}-\frac{n}{2\beta}-e\right]\;\!\mathrm{d}\xi\ =\ \ r^{-1}\!\!\!\!\!\underbrace{n\omega_{n}r^{n-1}}_{\text{area of sphere of radius $r$}},

so that

\displaystyle\log{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,n,e}

\displaystyle=\log n\omega_{n}+\frac{n-2}{2}\log\left(\frac{n}{\beta}+2e\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\log n\omega_{n}+\frac{n-2}{2}\log\frac{n}{\beta}}_{=:\,C(\beta,n)}\ +\ \frac{n-2}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{2\beta e}{n}\right),

and the convergence (
4.27
) follows from a Taylor development of the final term.

To prove part
2
, first note that the sequence
{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,n,e}^{-1}\zeta_{\beta,n,e}
of probability measures is not tight on
{\mathbf{H}}
(in fact its support tends to infinity in the
{\mathbf{H}}
-norm). However, applying the variance bound of Lemma
B.4
we observe that

\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,n,e}}\int_{\Xi_{n}}\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbf{X}}\zeta_{\beta,n,e}({\mathrm{d}}\xi)={\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{n,R_{n}}}\|{\mathbb{C}}^{1/2}\xi\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\leq C\frac{1}{\beta}\trace_{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbb{C}},

where we set
R_{n}:=\beta^{-1}+2e/n\to\beta^{-1}
.
Therefore the sequence is tight when considered as probability measures on
{\mathbf{X}}
, and along a subsequence the sequence converges narrowly to a probability measure
\zeta_{\beta}
on
{\mathbf{X}}
(e.g.
[
Bog18
, Th. 2.3.4]
).

To characterize the limit, note that for any
h\in\Xi_{k}
the equivalence-of-ensemble result of Lemma
B.3
implies that

\int_{\Xi_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{i\left(h\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}\zeta_{\beta,n,e}({\mathrm{d}}\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\beta}\|h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right).

Taking elements
h
of the form

h=h_{k}+h^{\perp}\in\left(\bigcup_{k\geq 1}\Xi_{k}\right)\oplus(\Xi^{\mathbf{H}})^{\perp},

(B.5)

we note that
h_{k}={\mathcal{Q}}h
, and since
h^{\perp}
vanishes on each
\Xi_{n}
we find

\int_{\Xi_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{i\left(h\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}\zeta_{\beta,n,e}({\mathrm{d}}\xi)=\int_{\Xi_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{i\left(h_{k}\middle|\xi\right)_{\mathbf{H}}}\zeta_{\beta,n,e}({\mathrm{d}}\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\beta}\|{\mathcal{Q}}h\|_{\mathbf{H}}^{2}\right).

Since the space in (
B.5
) is a subspace of
{\mathbf{V}}
and is dense in
{\mathbf{H}}
, by part
3
of Lemma
A.7
we recognize the limit
\zeta_{\beta}
as the push-forward measure
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta}
. This also implies that the whole sequence converges.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.14
.

The assertion of Lemma
4.14
follows from that of Lemma
4.15
by integration: for any
\varphi\in{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{b}}({\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}})
we have

\displaystyle\int\limits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}}\varphi(z,\eta)\,\mu_{\beta,n}({\mathrm{d}}z\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eqdef:mu-beta-n-e}}}{{=}}\int\limits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}\Xi^{\mathbf{X}}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}}\varphi(z,w+\xi)\,\mu_{\beta,n,e}({\mathrm{d}}z\;\!\mathrm{d}w\;\!\mathrm{d}\xi\;\!\mathrm{d}e)

\displaystyle\kern-5.0064pt\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits^{n\to\infty}}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,{\mathcal{E}}_{0}}}\int\limits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}\Xi^{\mathbf{X}}{\times}{\mathbb{R}}}\varphi(z,w+\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{\beta e}\,\bm{\updelta}\big[{\mathcal{H}}_{zw}(z,w)-{\mathcal{E}}_{0}+e\big]({\mathrm{d}}z\;\!\mathrm{d}w)\;(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta})({\mathrm{d}}\xi)\;\!\mathrm{d}e

\displaystyle\kern-17.58688pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\eqref{eqdef:pdelta}}}\frac{1}{{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta,{\mathcal{E}}_{0}}}\int\limits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}\Xi^{\mathbf{X}}}\varphi(z,w+\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{zw}(z,w)+\beta{\mathcal{E}}_{0}}\;(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta})({\mathrm{d}}\xi)\;\!\mathrm{d}z\;\!\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{Z}}_{\beta}}\int\limits_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{W}}{\times}\Xi^{\mathbf{X}}}\varphi(z,w+\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{-\beta{\mathcal{H}}_{zw}(z,w)}(\widehat{\mathbb{Q}}_{\#}\gamma_{\beta})({\mathrm{d}}\xi)\;\!\mathrm{d}z\;\!\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\kern-26.34389pt\mathrel{\mathop{=}\limits^{\eqref{char:Htot-Hzw-Q}}}\int_{{\mathbf{Z}}{\times}{\mathbf{X}}}\varphi(z,\eta)\mu_{\beta}({\mathrm{d}}z\;\!\mathrm{d}\eta).\qed

Appendix C
Dilations

The notion of dilations and compressions was introduced in Section
3.1
.
We now recall the existence of dilations.

Theorem C.1

(
[
SzF70
, Thm. 8.1]
)
.

Every strongly continuous contraction semigroup
(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathbf{G}}
admits a
dilation
(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on a Hilbert space
{\mathbf{H}}\supset{\mathbf{G}}
.

Moreover, there is a minimal dilation in the sense that

\mathrm{span}\Big(\bigcup\nolimits_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}S_{t}{\mathbf{G}}\Big)\ \text{ is dense in \ }{\mathbf{H}}.

Any two minimal dilations
\big({\mathbf{H}},(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
and
\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{H}},(\widetilde{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)

are isomorphic in the sense that there exists a Hilbert-space isomorphism
\Psi\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}
such
that
\widetilde{S}_{t}=\Psi S_{t}\Psi^{-1}
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

It turns out that even for finite-dimensional
{\mathbf{G}}
the dilation space
{\mathbf{H}}

will be infinite-dimensional as soon as
(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is not unitary, i.e.,
as soon as
C_{t}
is a non-trivial contraction. Moreover, the subspace
{\mathbf{G}}

cannot be contained in the domain of the generator of the unitary group

(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
, as we now show.

Remark C.2

(Domains of the generators)
.

The unitary group has a densely-defined generator

{\mathbb{J}}\colon\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}})\subset{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
which is skew-adjoint,
i.e.,
{\mathbb{J}}^{*}=-{\mathbb{J}}
. For
h_{0}\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}})
the function

h(t)=S_{t}h_{0}=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}}{h_{0}}
is differentiable and satisfies

\dot{h}(t)={\mathbb{J}}h(t)
.

The contraction semigroup can be written as
C_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
for a
generator
{\mathbb{D}}
satisfying
{\mathbb{D}}+{\mathbb{D}}^{*}\geq 0
. If
C_{t}
is not unitary,
then
{\mathbb{D}}
is not skew-adjoint and there exists a
g\in{\mathbf{G}}
such that

\gamma:=\left(g\middle|{\mathbb{D}}{g}\right)_{\mathbf{G}}>0
. We set
p(t)=\|\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}g\|^{2}
and find

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\dot{p}(t)=-2\gamma
. Assuming
g\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}})
, the
dilation property gives

\dot{p}(0)=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\|{\mathbb{P}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}}g\|^{2}\big|_{t=0}=2\left({\mathbb{P}}g\middle|{\mathbb{P}}{\mathbb{J}}g\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=2\left(g\middle|{\mathbb{J}}g\right)_{\mathbf{H}}=0
. This is a
contradiction, and we conclude
g\not\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}})
.

Hence,
{\mathbb{J}}
is an unbounded operator, and
{\mathbf{H}}
is
infinite-dimensional. If
\left(g\middle|{\mathbb{D}}g\right)_{\mathbf{G}}>0
for every
g\not=0
, then
we even have
\mathrm{dom}({\mathbb{J}})\cap{\mathbf{G}}=\{0\}
.

∎

In the context of this paper, it is advantageous to generalize the notion of dilation slightly.

Definition C.3

(Generalized dilation)
.

A triple
\big({\mathbf{H}},\Phi,(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is called a
generalized dilation
of the contraction
semigroup
\big({\mathbf{W}},(\widehat{C}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\big)
if
(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is a strongly continuous unitary group on
{\mathbf{H}}

and the linear map
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
satisfies

\Phi^{*}\Phi=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{W}}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi^{*}S_{t}\Phi=\widehat{C}_{t}\in\mathrm{Lin}({\mathbf{W}};{\mathbf{W}})\text{ \ for all }t\geq 0.

Note that from this definition we see that
{\mathbb{P}}=\Phi\Phi^{*}\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{H}}
is an orthogonal projection
onto
{\mathbf{G}}=\Phi{\mathbf{W}}\subset{\mathbf{H}}
. In particular, setting
C_{t}=\Phi\widehat{C}_{t}\Phi^{*}|_{\mathbf{G}}
the unitary
group
\big({\mathbf{H}},(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is a dilation of
\big({\mathbf{G}},(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}\big)
in the sense of
Definition
3.1
. However, since
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{G}}
is an isometry with inverse

\Phi^{*}|_{\mathbf{G}}
, the two contraction semigroups
(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\widehat{C}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are isomorphic. As
a consequence, all minimal dilations are isomorphic to any minimal generalized dilation, which is defined via
the minimality condition that
\bigcup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Phi\big(C_{t}{\mathbf{W}}\big)
is dense in
{\mathbf{H}}
.

C.1
An explicit construction of a generalized dilation

Here we repeat the explicit construction of a generalized dilation from
[
LaP89
, Thm. 2.1]

or
[
KüS84
, Sec. 1.2.3]
and give the full proofs because of its importance of compressions and dilations for our analysis.

We now write the contraction semigroup
\big({\mathbf{W}},(C_{t})_{t\geq 0}\big)
in the form
C_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}
. Moreover, we follow our assumptions in Section
2
and assume

\mathrm{dim}({\mathbf{W}})<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\exists\,\delta>0:\ \|\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}\|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\delta t}\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

(C.1)

To construct a generalized dilation we define

\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}:={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})\text{ with }\|f\|_{{\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\|f(y)\|_{\mathbf{W}}^{2}\;\!\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{G}}:=\big\{\,\Phi v\in\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\>\big|\>v\in{\mathbf{W}}\,\big\},

where the bounded linear operator
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{G}}\subset\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
is given by

(\Phi w)(y):=\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}w\,\bm{1}_{{]{-}\infty,0]}}(y),\quad\text{with }\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}:=({\mathbb{D}}{+}{\mathbb{D}}^{*})^{1/2},

(C.2)

with adjoint operator
\Phi^{*}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
,

\Phi^{*}f=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\,\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\,f(s)\;\!\mathrm{d}y
.

In contrast to
[
LaP89
,
KüS84
]
, we included the factor
\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}
into the mapping
\Phi
to obtain a
isometry in the given norm. For the convenience of the reader we provide the full proof that

\big(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},\Phi,({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is a generalized dilation for

\big({\mathbf{W}},(\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}})_{t\geq 0}\big)
.

Proposition C.4

(Explicit dilation)
.

Assume that
\big({\mathbf{W}},(\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}})_{t\geq 0}\big)
satisfies (
C.1
), define
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
,

{\mathbf{G}}
, and
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{G}}\subset{\mathbf{G}}
as above, and set

{\mathbb{P}}=\Phi\Phi^{*}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to{\mathbf{G}}\subset\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
.

1.

We have
\Phi^{*}\Phi={\mathbb{I}}_{\mathbf{W}}
,
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}^{2}={\mathbb{P}}^{*}
,
\mathrm{range}\,{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbf{G}}
.

In particular,
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
is an isometry and
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{G}}
is unitary with
inverse
\Phi^{*}|_{\mathbf{G}}
. Moreover,
{\mathbb{P}}
is the orthogonal projector from
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
onto
{\mathbf{G}}
.

2.

Defining the strongly continuous unitary shift group
({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})

via
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}f=f(\cdot-t)
the semigroup
\big({\mathbf{W}},(\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}})_{t\geq 0}\big)
has the generalized
dilation
\big(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},\Phi,({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
. Moreover, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

g\in{\mathbf{G}}=\Phi{\mathbf{W}}
we have the relation

{\mathbb{P}}\,{\mathbb{S}}_{t}g={\mathbf{R}}(t)g,\quad\text{where }{\mathbf{R}}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\Phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}\,\Phi^{*}&\text{for }t\geq 0,\\[-1.99997pt]
\Phi\,\mathrm{e}^{\,t\,{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\,\Phi^{*}&\text{for }t\leq 0.\end{array}\right.

(C.3)

Proof.

Part
1
. The property of an isometry is based on the standard relation

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\big(\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}\big)^{*}\,\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}\;\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}({\mathbb{D}}^{*}{+}{\mathbb{D}})\,\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}\;\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}y}\big(\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}\big)\;\!\mathrm{d}y={\mathbb{I}}_{\mathbf{W}}.

(C.4)

To establish
\Phi^{*}\Phi={\mathbb{I}}_{\mathbf{W}}
we simply calculate, for
w_{1},w_{2}\in{\mathbf{W}}
,

\displaystyle(\Phi w_{1}|\Phi w_{2})_{{\mathbf{H}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\big(\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}w_{1}\big|\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}w_{2}\big)_{\mathbf{W}}\;\!\mathrm{d}y=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\big(\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}w_{1}\big|w_{2}\big)_{\mathbf{W}}\;\!\mathrm{d}y\overset{\text{\eqref{eq:bbD.relation}}}{=}(w_{1}|w_{2})_{\mathbf{W}}.

With this and
{\mathbb{P}}=\Phi\Phi^{*}
the identities
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}={\mathbb{P}}
and
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}^{*}
follow immediately, i.e.,

{\mathbb{P}}
is an orthogonal projector. Moreover,
{\mathbb{P}}f=\Phi(\Phi^{*}f)
implies

\mathrm{range}\,{\mathbb{P}}\subset\mathrm{range}\,\Phi={\mathbf{G}}
, whereas
{\mathbb{P}}\Phi v=\Phi\Phi^{*}\Phi w=\Phi w
shows

\mathrm{range}\,{\mathbb{P}}\supset\mathrm{range}\,\Phi={\mathbf{G}}
.

Part
2
. Now we consider
t\geq 0
and
v\in{\mathbf{W}}
and obtain

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}\big({\mathbb{S}}_{t}\Phi w\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\,\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\big(\Phi w\big)(s{-}t)\;\!\mathrm{d}s=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\Sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathrm{e}^{(s-t){\mathbb{D}}}w\,\bm{1}_{]{-}\infty,0]}(s{-}t)\,\;\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}}\;\!\mathrm{d}s\ \mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}w\overset{\text{\eqref{eq:bbD.relation}}}{=}\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}}w.

Hence,
\big(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},\Phi,({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is a generalized dilation in the sense of
Definition
C.3
.

For
t\leq 0
we have
\bm{1}_{]{-}\infty,0]}(s{-}t)=0
for
s>t
and obtain

\displaystyle\Phi^{*}\big({\mathbb{S}}_{t}\Phi w\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\Sigma^{2}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(s-t){\mathbb{D}}}w\,\;\!\mathrm{d}s=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\!\!\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\mathrm{e}^{r{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{r{\mathbb{D}}}w\,\;\!\mathrm{d}s=\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}w.

Applying the operator
\Phi
to the two relations for
\Phi^{*}\big({\mathbb{S}}_{t}\Phi w\big)
(for
t\geq 0
and
t\leq 0
, respectively)
and replacing
\Phi w
by
g=\Phi w
such that
w=\Phi^{*}g
, we find the final
relation (
C.3
).
∎

The major advantage of this explicit generalized dilation is that it
introduces, in a very natural way, the time shift
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}

on the Hilbert space
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
which will take over the place of the linear evolution

\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}
. The specification to the time shift is advantageous as the time shift is also the
natural variable in the random processes
(Y_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
.

Remark C.5

(Minimality)
.

The above construction of a generalized dilation also allows us to show that the dilation is minimal. For
this we have to show that

\mathfrak{G}:=\mathrm{span}\big\{\,{\mathbb{S}}_{t}g\>\big|\>t\in{\mathbb{R}},\ g\in{\mathbf{G}}\,\big\}=\mathrm{span}\big\{\,{\mathbb{S}}_{t}(\Phi w)\>\big|\>t\in{\mathbb{R}},\ w\in{\mathbf{W}}\,\big\}

is dense in
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
. For
h\in{]0,1[}
and
w\in{\mathbf{W}}
, we define the functions

\Psi_{h,w}:={\mathbb{S}}_{h}\big(\Phi w)-\Phi w:\ y\mapsto\begin{cases}0&\text{for }y>h,\\
\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(y-h){\mathbb{D}}}w&\text{for }y\in{]0,h]},\\
\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\mathrm{e}^{y{\mathbb{D}}}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-h{\mathbb{D}}}{-}{\mathbb{I}}\big)w&\text{for }y\leq 0.\end{cases}

Clearly,
\Psi_{h,w}\in\mathfrak{G}
. Using
\mathrm{dim}({\mathbf{W}})<\infty
we have

\|\mathrm{e}^{-h{\mathbb{D}}}{-}{\mathbb{I}}\|\leq Ch
for
h\in[0,1]
and obtain

\big\|\Psi_{h,w}-w\bm{1}_{{[0,h[}}\big\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}\leq C_{*}h\|w\|_{\mathbf{W}},

with a constant
C_{*}
depending only on
{\mathbb{D}}
.

To show that
f\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
can be approximated by linear combinations
of
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}\Psi_{h,w}
, we fix
\varepsilon>0
and find a piecewise constant
\overline{f}_{N}\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
such that

\big\|f-\overline{f}_{N}\big\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}<\varepsilon/2\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{f}_{N}=\sum_{j=-N^{2}}^{N^{2}}w^{N}_{j}\bm{1}_{{[j/N,(j+1)/N[}}\ \text{ with }N\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and }w^{N}_{j}\in{\mathbf{W}}.

Here
N
can be made as large as desired, by refining the partition. We now approximate
\overline{f}
by

\widehat{f}_{N}=\sum_{-N^{2}}^{N^{2}}{\mathbb{S}}_{j/N}\Psi_{1/N,w^{N}_{j}}
, which clearly lies in
\mathfrak{G}
, and obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\big\|\overline{f}_{N}-\widehat{f}_{N}\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}^{2}=\sum_{j=-N^{2}}^{N^{2}}\big\|w_{j}^{N}\bm{1}_{{[j/N,(j+1)/N[}}-\overline{{\mathbb{S}}}_{j/n}\Psi_{1/N,w_{j}^{N}}\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\ \sum_{j=-N^{2}}^{N^{2}}C_{*}^{2}(1/N)^{2}\|w^{N}_{j}\|_{\mathbf{W}}^{2}\ =\ \frac{C_{*}}{N}\,\big\|\overline{f}_{N}\big\|^{2}_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}\ \leq\ \frac{C_{*}}{N}\,\big(\,\|f\|^{2}_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}{+}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\,\big)^{2}.

Choosing
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
sufficiently big, the last term is less than
\varepsilon^{2}/4
, and thus we conclude

\|f-\widehat{f}_{N}\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})}<\varepsilon
as desired. Hence,
\mathfrak{G}
is dense in

\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
.

∎

C.2
Continuous extension of the group
(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
from
{\mathbf{H}}
to
{\mathbf{X}}

In Assumption
2.3
we assumed that the unitary group

\big({\mathbf{H}},(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
describing the heat bath can be
extended to a strongly continuous group on the larger space
{\mathbf{X}}
, on which we
are able to realize the Gaussian measure
\gamma_{\beta}
for the
finite-temperature heat bath. We will show in Proposition
C.6

that this assumption is already a consequence of the dilation property from
Assumption
1.2
.

As a first step we construct the Gaussian measure
\overline{\gamma}_{\beta}
on an
extension
\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
of the special space
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
. This leads
to a classical white noise process, but now taking values in the
finite-dimensional Hilbert space
{\mathbf{W}}
. While translations
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}
can easily be
defined for the white noise process, we have to be more careful and construct a
Hilbert space
\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
on which we can extend the unitary group

\big(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
to a strongly continuous group

\big(\overline{{\mathbf{X}}},({\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
, which will be no longer
unitary. If
{\mathbb{N}}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
is the embedding operator, then the
extension property means

\forall\>t\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \forall\>h\in\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}:\quad{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}{\mathbb{N}}h={\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{S}}_{t}h.

(C.5)

As explained in Section
2.2
we further
need that
{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{N}}^{*}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
is trace class.

We give an explicit construction following the ideas in
[
Rey06
]
. We
define the self-adjoint operator

{\mathbb{A}}\colon\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})\subset\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
via

\displaystyle\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})=\big\{\,u\in{\mathrm{H}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})\>\big|\>t\mapsto(1{+}t^{2})u(t)\in{\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})\,\big\},

\displaystyle({\mathbb{A}}u)(t)=-\frac{1}{2}u^{\prime\prime}(t)+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\,u(t).

Here
{\mathrm{H}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
is the Sobolev space of measurable
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}
-functions

u\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
with two weak derivatives in
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}
. This is a
{\mathbf{W}}
-valued
version of the quantum harmonic oscillator, and hence is self-adjoint with the
discrete spectrum
\big\{\,n{-}1/2\>\big|\>n\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\big\}\subset{[1/2,\infty)}
, where
all eigenvalues have multiplicity
\dim({\mathbf{W}})<\infty
. Consequently
{\mathbb{A}}
is
an invertible operator, and its inverse
{\mathbb{A}}^{-1}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt
operator. This implies that
{\mathbb{A}}
satisfies the assumptions for the
construction in Section
A.2
, i.e. we can define

\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}=\mathrm{Dom}({\mathbb{A}})={\mathrm{H}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})\cap{\mathrm{L}}^{2}_{(2)}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}=\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}^{*}={\mathrm{H}}^{-2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})+{\mathrm{L}}^{2}_{(-2)}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}}),

where the subscripts “
(m)
” denote the
{\mathrm{L}}^{2}
space with measure

(1{+}t^{2})^{m}{\mathrm{d}}t
. In
\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}
we choose the graph norm
\|u\|_{\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}}=\|{\mathbb{A}}u\|_{\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}}
.

Concerning the shift group
(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},{\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
we observe that it can be
restricted to
\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}
, since
u\in\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}
implies
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}u\in\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}

for all
t
. Setting
{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{V}}_{t}={\mathbb{S}}_{t}|_{\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}}:\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}
we
have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{V}}_{t}u\|_{\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|({{\mathbb{S}}}_{t}u)^{\prime\prime}\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}}+\|(1{+}s^{2})({{\mathbb{S}}}_{t}u)(s)\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2C\big(\|u^{\prime\prime}\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}}+(1{+}t^{2})\|(1{+}r^{2})u(r)\|_{{\mathrm{L}}^{2}}\big)\leq(1{+}t^{2})C\|u\|_{\overline{{\mathbf{V}}}}.

Hence,
\big(\overline{{\mathbf{V}}},({\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{V}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
forms a strongly continuous group of shift
operators. By dualization (with respect to the duality in
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
) we can define the extension

{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{X}}};\ \ {\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}=\big({\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{V}}_{-t}\big)^{*}

which provides the strongly continuous group
\big(\overline{{\mathbf{X}}},({\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
satisfying
\|{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\|_{\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}}\leq C^{2}(1{+}t^{2})
.

Proposition C.6

(Assumption
2.3
holds)
.

If the contraction semigroup
\big({\mathbf{G}},(\mathrm{e}^{-t{\mathbb{D}}})_{t\geq 0}\big)

satisfies (
C.1
) and

\big({\mathbf{H}},(\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is an arbitrary minimal
dilation, then there exists a larger space
{\mathbf{X}}
with embedding

{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbf{H}}\to{\mathbf{X}}
and a strongly continuous group
(\widehat{S}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}

such

\displaystyle{\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{N}}^{*}\colon{\mathbf{X}}\to{\mathbf{X}}\quad\text{is trace class}

\displaystyle\forall\,t\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \forall\,h\in{\mathbf{H}}:\quad\widehat{S}_{t}{\mathbb{N}}h={\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}h.

Proof.

We use the explicit construction of the generalized dilation

\big(\overline{{\mathbf{H}}},({\mathbb{S}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
and of the explicit extension to

\big(\overline{{\mathbf{X}}},({\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
.

By Theorem
C.1
the two dilations are unitarily equivalent by a
unitary map
U\colon{\mathbf{H}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
. If
\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}
is the embedding of
\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}

into
\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
, then we can define

{\mathbf{X}}:=\overline{{\mathbf{X}}},\quad{\mathbb{N}}:=\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}\,U,\quad\widehat{S}_{t}={\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}.

Clearly,
{\mathbb{N}}{\mathbb{N}}^{*}=\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}U\,U^{*}\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}^{*}=\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}\,\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}^{*}
is
trace class by the explicit construction. Moreover,

\widehat{S}_{t}{\mathbb{N}}h={\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}Uh\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathbb{S}}_{t}Uh=\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}\,UU^{-1}{\mathbb{S}}_{t}Uh\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}{\mathbb{N}}\,\mathrm{e}^{t{\mathbb{J}}_{\mathcal{B}}}h

holds for all
h\in{\mathbf{H}}
. In
\overset{\text{(1)}}{=}
we used the explicit
extension of
{\mathbb{S}}^{\mathbf{X}}_{t}
of
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}
with embedding
\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}
, and in

\overset{\text{(2)}}{=}
we used the definition of
{\mathbb{N}}
and that the two
dilations are equivalent via the unitary operator
U
.
∎

The advantage of using the explicit generalized dilation involving

\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}={\mathrm{L}}^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbf{W}})
becomes even more apparent if we use the structure of
the mapping
\Phi\colon{\mathbf{W}}\to{\mathbf{G}}\subset\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}
and

\Phi^{*}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to{\mathbf{W}}
. Combining
\Phi^{*}
and the shift
{\mathbb{S}}_{t}
we
obtain the mapping

{\mathcal{U}}:\overline{{\mathbf{H}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{H}}};\quad({\mathcal{U}}f)(t):=\Phi^{*}\big({\mathbb{S}}_{t}f\big).

This corresponds to the mapping
t\mapsto Y_{t}(\eta_{0}):=\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\widehat{S}_{t}\eta_{0}
from Lemma
2.4
. We simply “dropped” the Hilbert-space
isomorphism
\Phi:{\mathbf{W}}\overset{1\text{-}1}{\longleftrightarrow}{\mathbf{G}}
.

We now show that
{\mathcal{U}}
has a bounded linear extension
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\colon\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}\to\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}
that is consistent with
Y_{t}
in Lemma
2.4

and is defined everywhere, i.e., we do not need the measurable extension

\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
defined
\gamma_{\beta}
a.e.
For this we observe that

\big({\mathcal{U}}f\big)(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}f(s{-}t)\;\!\mathrm{d}s.

Thus,
{\mathcal{U}}
is realized by convolution with the kernel
s\mapsto\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathrm{e}^{s{\mathbb{D}}^{*}}\bm{1}_{]-\infty,0]}
. In Fourier space the
convolution is realized by the multiplication with the Fourier multiplier

{\mathbb{R}}\ni\sigma\mapsto M(\sigma)^{*}:=\Sigma_{\mathbb{D}}\big({\mathbb{D}}^{*}-\mathrm{i}\sigma{\mathbb{I}}\big)^{-1}

which is smooth and bounded. As the operator
{\mathbb{A}}
stays invariant (up to
changing constants) under Fourier transform
\mathfrak{F}
, we also see that

\mathfrak{F}
maps
{{\mathbf{V}}}
into itself, and hence by duality also
{{\mathbf{X}}}
into

{{\mathbf{X}}}
. Clearly, multiplying by
M
defines a bounded linear operator in

{{\mathbf{V}}}
, because
M
lies in
{\mathrm{C}}^{2}_{\mathrm{b}}\big({\mathbb{R}};\mathrm{Lin}({\mathbf{W}})\big)
. By
duality the multiplication multiplying by
M^{*}
defines a bounded linear
operator in
{{\mathbf{X}}}={{\mathbf{V}}}^{*}
. This shows that there is a bounded linear extension

\widehat{\mathcal{U}}\colon{{\mathbf{X}}}\to{{\mathbf{X}}}
of
{\mathcal{U}}\colon{{\mathbf{H}}}\to{{\mathbf{H}}}
.
